Executive Summary



This Audit Report has been prepared in six chapters. Chapters I to V deal with Social,
General, Economic, Public Sector Undertakings, Revenue Sectors and Chapter VI deals
with Follow up of Audit observations.

This Report contains 28 audit paragraphs (including sixteen general paragraphs) and four
Performance Audits. According to the existing arrangements, copies of the draft
compliance audit paragraphs and draft performance audits were sent to the Secretary of
the Department concerned by the Principal Accountant General (Audit) with a request to
furnish replies within six weeks. Replies were received from the departments concerned
except in respect of five paragraphs.

Expenditure of हैं 4.21 crore was rendered infructuous due to improper survey and

injudicious selection of site for the construction of 575 bedded hospital.
(Paragraph 1.3)

Violation of guidelines resulted in irregular expenditure of % 6.48 crore due to excess
deployment of 648 teachers in schools while other schools faced shortage.
(Paragraph 1.4)

Government of India (GOI) introduced (March 2005) Rajiv Gandhi Grameen
Vidyutikaran Yojana with the objective of providing access to electricity to all Rural
Households and improving the rural electricity infrastructure. 90 per cent of the overall
cost of the projects was to be provided by GOI as capital subsidy. The balance project
cost was to be met by the Governments of respective States through loan from Rural
Electrification Corporation Limited. Cost of electrification of BPL households was,
however, to be subsidised fully by the GOI. Energy and Power Department, Government
of Sikkim was the implementing agency of the scheme in the State.

Under the scheme, the State targeted to electrify 25 un-electrified villages and intensive
electrification of 413 already electrified villages with free electricity connection to 12,081
BPL households by March 2009. The projects could not be completed even after four and
half years of scheduled completion time. As of November 2013 the EPD completed
electrification of all 25 un-electrified villages as well as intensive electrification of 383
villages which were already electrified and provided free electricity connection to 9,832
BPL households.

(Paragraph 2.3)



Concerned with the slow growth in the Agriculture and allied sectors, the National
Development Council in its meeting held on 29 May 2007, resolved to launch a special
Additional Central Assistance Scheme Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY) to
incentivise States to draw up plans for their agriculture sector more comprehensively.
RKVY aimed at achieving four per cent annual growth in the Agriculture sector during
the XI Plan period by ensuring a holistic development of Agriculture and allied sectors.
The main objectives of the scheme inter alia were: (i) to incentivise the States so as to
increase public investment in Agriculture and allied sectors; (ii) to achieve the goal of
reducing the yield gaps in important crops, through focused interventions; (iii) to bring
about quantifiable changes in the production and productivity of various components of
Agriculture and allied sectors by addressing them in a holistic manner.

In Sikkim, the Agriculture and allied sector had achieved four per cent annual growth rate
towards contribution to Gross State Domestic Product during the XI Plan period.
However, adequate and envisaged planning process for overall vision for development of
the district was absent both in the District Agriculture Plan and State Agriculture Plan
and it was coupled with flaws in the preparation of DPRs. Failure on the part of the Nodal
Department to furnish the UCs in time also led to the curtailment of funds. In cases of
production of major crops (i.e. Cereals, Pulses and Oil seeds), the four per cent growth
rate was achieved only in two years.

The smooth functioning of the scheme and achievement of its objectives was adversely
affected by improper fund management, delays, taking up of unviable projects and lack of
monitoring, review, evaluation and field studies from the State Level Selection
Committee.

(Paragraph 2.4)

Audit of ‘Execution of Suspension Foot Bridges (SFBs) under SIDF’ by the Rural
Management & Development Department through Zilla Panchayats revealed that out of
66 SFBs, 51 were completed and remaining 15 were under construction. Out of the
completed SFBs, 21 SFBs were completed recording a delay of more than six months.
The results of Audit on implementation of projects indicated defective survey and
estimation, irregularity in award of works, execution of works inconsistent with
technically sanctioned approved designs and estimates, excess and avoidable expenditure
on construction of SFBs with longer span than required, undue favour to contractors,
excess issue of material and unauthorised expenditure.

(Paragraph 2.5)

The Department violated the terms of sanction of the Government of India which resulted
in unauthorised expenditure of 92.40 lakh.
(Paragraph 2.6)



Unauthorised expenditure of = 187.08 lakh under Integrated Nutrient Management due
to non-adherence to cost norms as stipulated under the guidelines of the scheme.
(Paragraph 2.7)

Contractors were paid हें 1.97 crore for carriage of stones which was in excess of
requirement as stipulated in the AOR and in violation of the SPW Manual
(Paragraph 2.8)

Rejection of the highest offer for sale of power (30 MW) by the Energy and Power
Department without valid reason resulted in loss of € 2.55 crore besides loss of interest of
% 1.01 crore against unrecovered sale proceeds of € 7.14 crore.

(Paragraph 2.9)

Due to failure in insisting upon the Bank for honouring the Bank Guarantee in full, the
Company could not recover हैं 1.28 crore causing interest loss of € 51 lakh.
(Paragraph 3.2)

Levying of interest beyond the permissible limit by the Company resulted in extra
financial burden of € 38.64 lakh on the beneficiaries.
(Paragraph 3.3)

Delay in assessment of tax for the period 2006-07 to 2010-11 on sales turnover of
% 142.80 crore benefitted a company as it retained tax collected from the consumers
amounting to हैं 28.56 crore over a period exceeding two to seven years.

(Paragraph 4.5)

Irregular utilisation of Way Bills resulted in evasion of SVAT amounting to % 1.09 crore.
(Paragraph 4.6)

Systemic lacunae in the Commercial Taxes Division to ensure realisation of taxes from
works contractors executing works of registered dealers resulted in loss of revenue of
% 38.21 lakh.

(Paragraph 4.7)



The Audit on Disaster Management indicated that the State, despite being located in a
seismically active zone and being a victim of recurrent natural disasters, was yet to
adequately recognise these disasters as threats which thwarted its developmental plans
and mission to transform itself into a model State within a targeted time frame. The
Disaster Management Act 2005 and other guidelines issued by the GOI from time to time
covering all issues of Disaster Management (DM) had not been implemented fully. The
institutional mechanism to address DM issues had not been firmly established and the
DM policy 2007 not fully implemented. Further, the State DM plans require
strengthening and the Departmental DM plans were not made till the date of audit
(September 2013). There had been diversion from funds provided by GOI for immediate
relief and long-term reconstruction of damaged infrastructure.

The lack of urgency to address the issue of disasters is underscored by the fact that even
after more than seven years of enactment of the DM Act 2005 by the GOI, the State had
not laid down a proper institutional framework with appropriate manpower having
designated roles and responsibilities to address DM in the State. The apex body, State
DM Authority (SDMA) met only once during the five year period 2008-13, that too, only
to discuss relief activities after the earthquake of 18 September 2011. DM had not been
incorporated in the development planning process of the State. No financial resources had.
been earmarked in the State’s Plan towards DM. The State entirely depended on Central
support both for immediate relief activities as well as permanent reconstruction of
damaged infrastructure.

However, after the earthquake of 18 September 2011, the Government has initiated
various policy resolutions and capacity building measures which were expected to
strengthen DM in the State.

(Paragraph 5.3)

Sikkim became part of North East Region in April 1998 and availed the benefit of
funding from NLCPR and NEC. A number of infrastructural projects were constructed
out of these funds in various sectors such as Roads & Bridges, Irrigation & Flood
Control, Energy & Power, Human Resource Development Department, etc. The overall
responsibility of managing NLCPR and NEC funds is primarily vested with the
Development Planning, Economic Reforms and North Eastern Council Affairs
Department. The projects relating to Roads & Bridges are implemented by Roads &
Bridges Department.

The Audit of Roads & Bridges projects funded under NLCPR and NEC for the period
2008-13 was carried out during July-August 2013. The results of audit disclosed that out



of a total of 44 projects, only 18 (41 per cent) were completed. None of the projects were
completed within the scheduled dates. The physical progress of incomplete projects
ranged between 11 and 98 per cent as of March 2013. The completed projects (18) also
recorded a delay in completion ranging between one and 12 years beyond the scheduled
date of completion. This was partly due to delayed initiation of tender procedure,
defective preparation of DPRs, frequent change in scope of works, cost revision, short
release of matching share by State Government, etc. Similarly, out of total funds of
% 246.91 crore released during 2008-13, ~ 218.03 crore was incurred during 2008-13. Out
of = 218.03 crore spent, irregular and excess expenditure of = 19.27 crore were noticed in
Audit. The unspent funds coupled with irregular excess expenditure and slow pace of
implementation of projects indicated that the speedy development of infrastructure
projects was not achieved to the desired extent.

(Paragraph 5.4)

Source: Central Plan Scheme Monitoring System

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level of
delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls, etc.

After completion of audit of each unit on a test check basis, Inspection Reports containing
audit findings are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments are to furnish
replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Inspection Reports.
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled based on reply/action
taken or further action is required by the auditee for compliance. Some of the important
audit observations arising out of these Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in
the Audit Reports, which are submitted to the Governor of the State under Article 151 of
the Constitution of India for laying on the table of the Legislature.

The test audits were conducted involving expenditure of % 686.52 crore (including
expenditure of ~ 374.05 crore of previous years) (details of year-wise break-up is given
in Appendix 1.1) of the State Government under Social Sector. This Chapter contains
two Compliance Audit Paragraphs as given below:



For upgradation and modernisation of Sir Thutob Namgyal Memorial (STNM) Hospital,
Gangtok, the Health Care, Human Services & Family Welfare Department invited
(January 2008) ‘expression of interest’ from various firms of which five firms responded.
This was done without calling for tenders or through advertisement. The detailed
‘expression of interest’ submitted by these five firms were scrutinised by the Department
and M/s Archtech Consultants Private Limited (ACPL), Kolkata was selected (May 2008)
on the basis of recommendations of the Technical Committee constituted (April 2008) for
this purpose. The ACPL was entrusted with the preparation of Detailed Project Report
(DPR) along with Architectural Structure and Services Designing for 500 bedded Multi
Speciality Hospital at STNM complex, with the approval (May 2008) of the Cabinet.
The agreement was signed (June 2008) with the firm for the above works at a total fee of
% 6 crore (six per cent of the initial indicative project cost of € 100 crore).

Subsequently, the project at STNM complex was cancelled on the ground of insufficiency
of space and a revised proposal of the Department for consturction of a 575 bedded Multi
Speciality Hospital at Sokeythang (Tadong), through Building and Housing Department
(BHD), was approved (August 2008) by the Cabinet. Without calling for tenders, a
corrigendum to the agreement with ACPL was issued (March 2009) for preparation of
DPR for construction of a 575 bedded hospital at the new site of Sokeythang. An amount
of € 4.21 crore was paid to the firm till date (July 2013).

The project was again shifted from Sokeythang to Sochyagang (Sichey) on the ground
that there were public protests to the project at the earlier site. After inviting fresh tenders
through advertisement, a separate agreement was drawn up (August 2010) with M/s Civil
Engineers Enterprises Private Limited, Kolkata (CEEPL) through BHD for Drawing,
Design and Execution of 575 bedded Multi Speciality Hospital at Sochyagang at a cost of
₹ 459.48 crore on turnkey basis. Clause 12 of the agreement regarding consultancy fee
stipulated that the project cost would include planning, drawing, designing, etc., at a cost
of & 27.57 crore, being six per cent of the total project cost at Sochyagang. The BHD
confirmed (January 2013) that the documents prepared and services rendered by ACPL at
Sokeythang were not utilised for the hospital at Sochyagang, which was being
constructed by CEEPL.

Sikkim Financial Rules (SFR) also provide that the contracts should be placed only after
tenders had been widely invited.



It was observed that the Department failed to follow the provisions of SFR while placing
order with ACPL for the project at STNM complex. Thus, the reasonability of the rate
and availing of the best technical expertise for consultancy was not ensured through open.
competitive tenders. Further, it was also observed that the Department had not conducted
due diligence in ensuring suitability of the site for the hospital project duly ensuring
availability of adequate land, approach road and conducting public hearings, etc., at
Sokeythang.

Thus, an amount of ®% 4.21 crore was paid to ACPL for a project where not a single brick
had been laid at the site at Sokeythang, and was thereafter scrapped, thus rendering this
entire amount infructuous.

The Department stated (September 2013) that the approach road to the site at Sokeythang
for the Multi Specialty Hospital passed through the road leading to the ICAR complex. A
wider approach road essential for the hospital was lacking. The ICAR authority did not
agree for the widening of the road and moreover, the private land owners also declined to
sell additional land required for accommodating the hospital complex including quarters.

However, the reasons given for shifting of the site are all issues that should have been
sorted out before awarding the contract and those issues should have been resolved first.
It was observed that the DPR was contracted out even without ascertaining the
adequacy/suitability of the site and its approach road which rendered the expenditure of
₹ 4.21 crore infructuous.

The Government of Sikkim notified the Manpower Management Guidelines in February
2010 for determination of teacher strength in schools. It was stated therein that teacher
strength as given in the Schedule against each school shall be strictly adhered to and
under no circumstances would it be permissible to have more numbers on its roll than that
prescribed. It further stated that it shall be the bounden duty of the Heads of schools and
Drawing and Disbursing Officers to ensure that the salary of non-existent or excess
teachers over and above that prescribed were not issued and in the event of failure to do
so, would be realised from their monthly salary.

Test-check of 438 schools (out of 530 schools) for the period 2012-13 of East, North and
South districts revealed (February 2013) that there were 648 excess teachers in 254
schools against the prescribed norms, in violation of the Government instructions issued
by the Government.



Scrutiny of records further revealed (February 2013) that the deployment of teachers in
the Department was irrational and without reference to the guidelines issued by the
Government in this respect. While there were 648 excess teachers in 254 schools, there
were shortages of 198 teachers in 78 schools. The overall position of deployment was as
under:

Table 1.4.1

Net excess (+) /shortage (-) 308 | 1 | | 159 | | -18 | 450

*Total with regard to the sanctioned strength of each school

Thus, there was excess deployment of 648 teachers out of which 198 teachers could have
been re-deployed in the schools where there were shortage of teachers. Inaction by the
Department in this regard resulted in irregular expenditure of % 4.50 crore! and
injudicious expenditure of हैं 1.98 crore” besides adversely affecting the quality of
education being imparted in 198 schools.

The matter was reported to the Department/Government (May 2013); their reply had not
been received (October 2013).

Audit process starts with the assessment of risks faced by various departments of
Government based on expenditure incurred, criticality/complexity of activities. level of
delegated financial powers, assessment of overall internal controls, etc.

After completion of audit of each unit on a test check basis, Inspection Reports containing
audit findings are issued to the heads of the departments. The departments are to furnish
replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Inspection Reports.
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled based on reply/action
taken or further action is required by the auditee for compliance. Some of the important
audit observations arising out of these Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in
the Audit Reports, which are submitted to the Governor of the State under Article 151 of
the Constitution of India for laying on the table of the Legislature.

The test audits were conducted involving expenditure of = 1,166.34 crore (including
expenditure of ¥ 633.58 crore of previous years) (details of year-wise break-up is given in
Appendix 2.1) of the State Government under Economic Sector. This Chapter contains
one Performance Audit on ‘Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana’ and five Compliance Audit
Paragraphs as given below:

Government of India (GOI) introduced (March 2005) Rajiv Gandhi Grameen
Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY) with the objective of providing access to electricity to all
Rural Households (RHHs) and improving the rural electricity infrastructure. Ninety per
cent of the overall cost of the projects was to be provided by GOI as capital subsidy. The
balance project cost was to be met by the Governments of respective States through loan
from REC. Cost of electrification of BPL households was, however, to be subsidised fully



by the GOI. Energy and Power Department, Government of Sikkim was the implementing
agency of the scheme in the State.

Under the scheme, the State targeted to electrify 25 un-electrified villages and intensive
electrification of 413 already electrified villages with free electricity connection to 12,081
BPL households by March 2009. The projects could not be completed even after four and
a half years of scheduled completion time. As of November 2013, the EPD completed
electrification of all 25 un-electrified villages as well as intensive electrification of 383
villages which were already electrified and provided free electricity connection to 9,832
BPL households.

Following are the main highlights of the Audit:

(Paragraph 2.3.3.1)

(Paragraph 2.3.3.2 and 2.3.3.6)

(Paragraph 2.3.3.9)

(Paragraph 2.3.3.10)

(Paragraph 2.

2.3.1.1 Electrification is regarded as a vital necessity for the development of rural areas.
Hence, rural electrification has been a key component of various development schemes.
As per Rural Electrification Programme (2003-04) of Government of India (GOI),
definition of village electrification was revised (February 2004). According to the revised
definition, a village would be declared electrified if basic infrastructures such as
distribution transformer and distribution lines are provided in the inhabited locality;



electricity is provided to public places like schools, panchayat offices, health centres,
dispensaries, community centres, etc.; and, the number of households electrified should
be at least 10 per cent of the total number of households in the village.

The National Electricity Policy formulated (February 2005) by the GOI inter alia States
that the key objective of the development of the power sector is to supply electricity to all
areas including rural areas as mandated under Section 6 of the Electricity Act, 2003. Both
the GOI and the State Governments would jointly endeavour to achieve this objective.
GOI introduced (March 2005) Rajiv Gandhi Grameen Vidyutikaran Yojana (RGGVY)
with the objective of providing access to electricity to all Rural Households (RHHs) and.
improving the rural electricity infrastructure. The scheme was to be implemented during
2005-2009 (by March 2009) under the 10" and 117 plan periods (viz., 2002-07 and
2007-12) with the following objectives:

» establishing the Rural Electricity Distribution Backbone by providing 33/11KV
(or 66/11KV) Power Sub Stations (PSS) of adequate capacity;

v

creation of Village Electrification Infrastructure i.e., electrification! of un-
electrified villages/habitations and provision of distribution transformers of
appropriate capacity;

> catering to the requirement of electricity for agriculture and other activities
including irrigation pump sets, small and medium industries, healthcare,
education, etc. to facilitate overall rural development, employment generation and
poverty alleviation; and

Vv

electrification of un-electrified Below Poverty Line (BPL) households with 100
per cent capital subsidy.

2.3.1.2. Rural Electrification Corporation Limited (REC) was the nodal agency for
implementation of the scheme. As per the scheme, 90 per cent of the overall cost of the
projects was to be provided by GOI as capital subsidy. The balance project cost was to be
met by the Governments of respective States through loan from REC. Cost of
electrification of BPL households was, however, to be subsidised fully by the GOI.

2.3.1.3 The RGGVY Scheme in the State aimed (December 2006) at electrification of
438 villages during 10" and 11" Five-year plan periods covering 28,166 rural households
(including 11,458 BPL households) at an aggregate cost of € 57.10 crore. The estimated
cost of the project was subsequently revised (January 2009) to = 148.91 crore. Additional
quantity variation to the tune of %47.63 crore was further approved (February 2012) in
order to provide electricity to 623 left over/additional BPL households. Thus, the revised
coverage of the Scheme included 438 villages and 28,789 rural households (including
12,081? BPL households) at a total cost of = 196.54 crore.

As per new definition (2004), village would be declared electrified if basic infrastructures such as distribution transformer and
distribution lines are provided in the inhabited locality as well as the Dalit Basti/Hamlets; electricity is provided to public places
like schools, panchayat offices, health centres, dispensaries, community centres, etc.; and, the number of households electrified
should be at least 10 per cent of the total number of households in the village.

In the Ministry of Power, Government of India, data, BPL coverage in the North District is erroneously shown as 774 instead of 747.



2.3.1.4 The Energy & Power Department (EPD), Government of Sikkim (GoS) was the
implementing agency of the scheme in the State of Sikkim. The Principal Chief Engineer
cum Secretary, EPD is in charge of implementation of the scheme in the State. Senior
Chief Engineer (HQ) was appointed (March 2006) as a Nodal Officer of the RGGVY
Scheme, who was assisted by three Chief Engineers, three Additional Chief Engineers
and six Superintending Engineers at the circle level to supervise the field work.

2.3.1.5 The audit involved detailed scrutiny of records maintained at Energy and Power
Department, GoS as well as beneficiary surveys.

2.3.1.6 Simple Random Sampling without Replacement (SRSWOR) was adopted for
test check at all four levels i.e. Project Level, Block level, Village Level and Beneficiary
Level. At the project level, two out of four Districts (viz. East and West Districts) were
selected on the basis of risk criteria. For the purpose of beneficiary survey, four out of
five blocks in selected Districts (viz. Gangtok and Pakyong Blocks in East District and
Gyalshing and Soreng Blocks in West District) were selected. In each selected block, the
surveys of five villages’ were conducted. In each of the villages so selected, five BPL
beneficiaries were surveyed. Details are given below:

2.3.1.7 The audit objectives were to assess whether:

> the EPD had fixed targets in line with the sanctioned scheme and actual
achievement was consistent with the targets;

Vv

an efficient and effective plan for implementation of RGGVY scheme was
devised and implemented;

Vv

funds received for implementation of Scheme were put to effective use:

Vv

an effective monitoring and supervision mechanism was in place; and

5 ‘Three un-electrified and two electrified villages.



> the intended objectives of RGGVY were achieved and evaluation was done to find
out how far rural populace was benefited.

2.3.1.8 The criteria adopted for assessing the achievement of the scheme objectives
were derived from the following sources:

> The RGGVY scheme issued by GOI in March 2005 and February 2008;
Scheme guidelines issued by GOI/REC;

Rural Electrification Policy (August 2006);

Agreements entered into between EPD and REC ;

Detailed Project Reports (DPRs) of the scheme projects; and

VVVV WV

Agreements entered into by the EPD with the contractors and consultancies.

2.3.1.9 The methodology adopted for attaining the audit objectives with reference to
audit criteria consisted of explaining audit objective to top management in entry
conference (April 2013), analysis of data/records with reference to audit criteria, scrutiny
of records of the EPD relating to DPRs of scheme projects, receipt of funds, award of
works and their execution, monthly progress reports/ returns, interaction with the
management, issue of audit queries and draft report to the EPD for comments and final
discussion of audit findings with EPD/GoS in the exit conference (September 2013). In
addition, field survey of RGGVY Distribution Network along with BPL rural households
was also conducted so as to assess the impact of scheme implementation. The replies
received (August 2013) from EPD as well as the views expressed by the representatives
of EPD/GoS have been taken into consideration while finalising the Report.

2.3.2.1 The findings emerging from audit are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

was comparatively lower during remaining years mainly due to non-completion of works
as per schedule as brought out in the succeeding paragraphs.

State level Rural Electrification Plan

2.3.3.1. The MoP, GOI notified (August 2006) the Rural Electrification Policy with the
aim of providing access to electricity to all households by the year 2009. As per the Rural
Electrification Policy, the State Governments need to prepare and notify a Rural
Electrification Plan (RE Plan) within six months of notification of the Rural
Electrification Policy (viz. by February 2007). The requirement of notifying the RE plan
by State Governments in consultation with MoP was reiterated in the office memorandum
(OM) issued (February 2008) by MoP, GOI on sanction of continuation of RGGVY in the
11" Five year plan. The RE Plan was to be the roadmap for generation, transmission,
sub-transmission and distribution of electricity in a State so as to ensure achievement of
the objectives of the scheme within the stipulated time frame. As per the said OM of
MoP, in case the RE plan of any State is not notified within the stipulated period, the
capital subsidy provided under the scheme was to be converted into interest bearing loan.
MoP issued (April 2008) draft template for RE plan which included plans to energise
villages being electrified under RGGVY and strengthening of sub-transmission system to
cater to additional load after electrification of villages.

Excluding total interest income of & 3.91 crore earned by EPD against investment of scheme funds during 2008-09 to 2012-13
(viz. 70.13 crore in 2008-09, 70.21 crore in 2009-10, 70.14 crore in 2010-11, 71.60 crore in 2011-12 and @ 1.83 crore in

2012-13).



It was observed that the EPD could submit (January 2012) the draft RE Plan after a delay
of 60 months from the date (February 2007) stipulated under Rural Electrification Policy.
The draft RE Plan was, however, not approved by MoP as it failed to cover several
important aspects like upgrading of sub-transmission and transmission system, investment
plan, provision of revenue subsidy by State, etc. EPD revised the RE Plan and re-
submitted the same in October 2012. The revised RE Plan was, however, pending for
approval by MoP (November 2013).

Thus, in absence of approved RE Plan, the State could not ensure development of
adequate rural electricity infrastructure. Further, failure to notify RE plan exposed the
EPD against the risk of capital subsidy being converted into loan under RGGVY.

EPD stated (August 2013) that RE plan was submitted to REC during 2007 and was
rejected on the ground that it did not cover entire rural areas of the State. The RE Plan
was re-submitted (October 2012) to MoP for approval and same was yet to be received
(November 2013).

The reply is contrary to the facts as draft RE plan was originally submitted to MoP only
in January 2012 and EPD could not provide documents in support of their contention.
Failure to notify RE Plan had led to non-completion of the project even after seven years
of sanction (December 2006) of the Scheme (November 2013).

Formulation of RGGVY Scheme and preparation of DPR

2.3.3.2 For implementation of the Scheme in the State, a Bipartite Agreement was
executed (August 2005) between REC and EPD. Under the agreement, responsibility for
project formulation and its implementation in the identified areas involving planning,
design, engineering and procurement was entrusted to the EPD.

As per the directives of GOI, EPD was required to submit the Detailed Project Reports
(DPRs) in respect of four Districts of Sikkim to GOI by September 2005. EPD could,
however, submit (March 2006) the DPRs after a delay of six months, which were
approved in December 2006. The delay in submission of DPRs by EPD was attributable
mainly to late submission of basic data by the field offices.

Scrutiny of records revealed (May 2013) that the DPRs prepared by EPD had several
deficiencies as discussed below:

> Though the electricity connections to rural households were partially proposed to be
given out of the existing infrastructure, no assessment of the spare capacity
available with the existing infrastructure was conducted. Further, no estimates were
prepared to determine the quantum of additional energy required to cater to the
needs of the new connections (BPL and APL households proposed). Thus, the DPRs
were prepared without exhaustive field survey so as to identify the surplus
infrastructure available and actual energy requirements.

> The DPRs considered electrification of 64 out of 123 un-electrified schools. The



remaining 59 un-electrified schools were omitted to be covered under the RGGVY
scheme in the DPRs due to improper survey.

v

Based on the criteria laid down in Tenth Five-Year Plan (2002-2007), Department
of Economics, Statistics, Monitoring & Evaluation (DESME), GOS, had listed out
(October 2010) 21,618 households under BPL category in the State. The DPRs
prepared by EPD, however, reckoned total number of BPL households in the State
of Sikkim as 34,784 which was higher by 61 per cent of the BPL households as
identified by DESME.

» Subsequent revisions in the number of eligible BPL Households as well as the
quantum of work involved indicated that the initial DPRs were not realistic and
were prepared without proper field survey.

The above deficiencies in the DPRs ultimately led to inadequate provision of
infrastructure for rural power supply and shortfall in achieving 100 per cent village
electrification within the scheduled period.

EPD replied (August 2013) that DPRs were prepared based on field survey by the
departmental engineers and the methodology adopted for selection of BPL households
was as per the recommendations of the panchayats duly authorised by the Block
Development Officers concerned. It was further stated that all un-electrified schools were
proposed for coverage under the Scheme.

The reply does not adequately address the issue of adopting higher figures of BPL
households without reconciling the differences with the figures available with GoS. Since
the Government already had an official list of BPL households, the same should have
been adopted for implementation of the scheme. As regards electrification of schools, the
documents available with audit confirm that 59 out of 123 un-electrified schools remained.
out of the RGGVY coverage.

Absence of Business Plan and Financial Analysis

2.3.3.3 As per the guidelines prescribed by REC, the DPR should contain the Business
Plan and Financial Analysis of the project providing the category-wise details about
anticipated number of consumers, connected load, anticipated sale of energy, operation
and maintenance expenses, etc. for the next 15 years of operation so as to evaluate
sustainability of the proposed project. The DPRs submitted by the EPD did not, however,
contain any such Business Plan and Financial Analysis. Hence, in absence of Business
Plan, EPD failed to properly assess the additional energy requirement against the
infrastructure created under RGGVY and its revenue sustainability.

EPD stated (August 2013) that being a Government Department, they were not required
to formulate a Business Plan. The reply is contrary to the requirements of the REC project
formulation guidelines which prescribed for submission of Business Plan and Financial
Analysis by the project implementing agency with complete details of projected cash
inflow and outflow for next 15 years.



2.3.3.4 For the implementation of RGGVY Scheme in the State, the EPD bifurcated the
work into two parts, viz., (i) execution of electrification works upto Low Tension (LT)
distribution pole; and (ii) providing of connections from LT distribution pole to BPL
households. The works of electrification upto the LT distribution pole was executed
through turnkey contractors whereas the works of providing of connection from
distribution pole to BPL Households was executed by EPD departmentally.

2.3.3.5 For execution of works upto the LT distribution pole through tumkey
contractors, the entire scheme in the State was divided into eight packages” under four
districts and awarded to the lowest bidders after inviting open tenders at an aggregate cost
of % 178.11 crore®. As per the Letter of Award (LoA), the contractors were to complete
the work within 18 months of award of work. The package wise details of the work, value
of the contract (pre-quantity variation), actual cost and time taken are detailed in
Appendix 2.3.1.

It could be seen from the Appendix that out of the eight packages scheduled for
completion by August 2010, only three packages could be completed as of November
2013 after delays ranging from 9 months to 15 months. Remaining five packages were
pending for completion (November 2013) even after delay of 39 months from the
scheduled date. Delays in execution of packages by the contractors correspondingly
contributed towards delay in overall implementation of the Scheme in the State.

Reasons for the delay in completion of works are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

Surveys and submission of quantity variation proposal

2.3.3.6 As per the RGGVY milestones set by MoP, detailed surveys to assess
requirement of quantities against sanctioned scheme works were to be completed within
six months of award (February 2009) of work i.e., by August 2009. Due to delay in
conducting surveys, EPD could submit (November 2010) quantity variation proposal after
a delay of 14 months. The quantity variation proposal was, however, approved by GOI
after taking further period of 14 months in February 2012 at an estimated cost of हैं 47.63
crore as detailed in Appendix 2.3.2. EPD took another six months in issuing (August -
September 2012) the LoAs for quantity variation to the tumkey contractors on the terms
and conditions identical with the original LoA. The LoA had stipulated a completion
period of 12 months. The entire additional works against quantity variation were,
however, in progress (November 2013).

1 in Bast, 2 in West, 3 in North & 2 in South districts.
Including quantity variation (हैं 47.63 crore) approved in February 2012 after netting off the overhead and agency charges
payable to EPD/REC.



Thus, there was overall delay of 34 months in commencement of quantity variation works
due to the avoidable delays involved at each stage. This delay has jeopardised the
implementation of RGGVY scheme in the State.

Delay in acquisition of land

2.3.3.7 Under RGGVY, it was proposed to install twelve’ 11/11 KV switching sub-
stations (SSs) in the State to provide reliable and quality power. Work Orders for
construction and commissioning of these SSs were issued in February 2009 with
stipulation to complete the same within 18 months upto August 2010.

As per the milestone set by REC, the EPD was to hand over the required land to the
turnkey contractor within six months from the date of award of work. It was, however,
noticed that the EPD could handover land within the scheduled time only in respect of 2
out of 12 SSs. The land required for installation of remaining SSs was handed over to the
contractors after delays ranging from 3 to 21 months mainly due to excessive time taken
in finalisation of land acquisition process.

As a result, only 1!° SS out of the 12 SSs could be commissioned till November 2013 and
remaining 11 SSs were pending for commissioning (November 2013) even afier expiry of
39 months from the scheduled date. Thus, undue delay in finalising the land acquisition
process had defeated the scheme objectives of providing reliable and quality power
supply to the consumers.

It was further observed that ignoring the slow progress of works relating to construction
and commissioning of SSs, the turnkey contractors procured (May 2009) equipment
worth = 18 crore for energisation of 555. The equipment so procured by EPD, however,
remained idle due to delay in commissioning of the SSs. Thus, due to lack of
synchronization by EPD in two activities, funds invested in procurement of equipment
remained blocked for 42 months (November 2013).

While accepting the delay in acquisition and handing over the land, EPD stated (August
2013) that although there was delay in completion of SSs, the Government did not incur
any amount on cost escalation. The reply was, however, silent on blocking up of fund on
procurement of equipment. The reply further confirms the fact of depriving the
beneficiaries of availing the scheme benefits in time.

Delay in supply of materials and commencement of erection works

2.3.3.8 As per the schedule for RGGVY works, the turnkey contractors were to
commence supply of materials within six months of award of work and complete the
same within 15 months of work award. Further, the works relating to erection of poles
and stringing of conductors were also to begin within six months after award of work.

° 4 in East, 4 in West, 3 in South & 1 in North

It was noticed that 7 out of 8 contractors commenced delivery of materials after delays
ranging from 1'! to 13 months’? from scheduled date with corresponding delays in
completion of supplies by 13 months to 28 months. Delay in commencement and
completion of supplies had correspondingly delayed the execution of the erection works.

Delay in award of works

2.3.3.10 | GOI accorded (June 2006) in-principle approval to the DPRs submitted by the
EPD for implementation of the scheme. EPD conducted bidding for the RGGVY works in
two phases, viz., submission of Expression of Interest (Eol) for meeting qualification
requirements (QR) followed by techno-commercial bids.

It was noticed that EPD took eight months in appointment (February 2007) of consultants
for preparation of package-wise tender documents and invited Eol only in August 2007.
Although Eol was submitted by prospective bidders in September 2007, EPD took
another seven months in issue (May 2008) of bid documents to the successful bidders of
QR. Price bids were opened in August 2008 and the works were finally awarded
(February 2009) after approval (January 2009) by REC.

Thus, there was overall time gap of 30 months between the date of in-principle approval
(June 2006) and issue of work order (February 2009). The excessive time taken in award
of work correspondingly delayed implementation of RGGVY in Sikkim besides causing

significant cost over-run of 91.81 crore’® in the project implementation.

EPD stated (August 2013) that delay in award of works was due to administrative reasons
on the part of REC in approving rates of 110 per cent above the sanctioned costs quoted
by the 1.1 bidders. Reply is not in conformity with the facts as the REC had approved the
LI rates within five months period in January 2009 after opening (August 2008) of price
bids by EPD.

Procurement of materials at exorbitant rates

2.3.3.11 | Schedule of Rates (SOR) is a vital element in establishing the reasonableness
of prices. Adoption of prevailing SOR further ensures that the prices are worked out in a
realistic and objective manner on the basis of prevailing market rates.

During February 2009, EPD procured various items of material at SOR 2008 rates for use
in the maintenance work carried out departmentally. The material so procured mainly
includes steel lattice structure, MS channel, etc. Scrutiny of records revealed that work
order for similar works under RGGVY was awarded (February 2009) to contractors on
turnkey basis at 200 per cent above the SOR 2008 which was significantly higher than the
cost of works carried out departmentally by EPD during same period. For instance, the
rate of 25 KVA Distribution Transformer as per SOR 2008 was % 55,800 whereas the
same under RGGVY LOA was हें 1,68,286 (201 per cent above the SOR). There was no
justification on record for allowing the higher rates for similar works under RGGVY. The
total amount on procurement of items in excess of the SOR 2008 rates worked out to
₹ 28.60 crore.

EPD stated (August 2013) that the works were awarded based on the lowest quotations.
The factors like inflation of iron & steel prices, equipments, etc. were the reasons for high
rates. Reply is not convincing in view of the fact that similar nature of materials relating
to other works were procured by the EPD at the rates lower than the RGGVY works.

Different rates for identical items

2.3.3.12 Analysis of item-wise rates in respect of high value items incorporated in the
LoA of RGGVY works of all four Districts revealed that different ex-factory rates
(excluding freight & insurance) were allowed for supply of the same material with
identical specification in all eight packages. There was no recorded justification for
allowing different rates in different turnkey contracts for similar specification of material.
Few instances of dissimilarity noticed in the price of high value material of same
specifications are illustrated below:

Audit examined cost variations of high value items for review and arrived at the financial
loss by comparing item wise rates having the same specification in respect of all turnkey
contractors executing RGGVY Scheme. The difference in unit rates worked out to
% 16.25 crore. The variations in the rate of materials were due to absence of proper
coordination and comparative analysis of the price quotations received against each of the
eight packages.

The EPD stated (August 2013) that for East District, the best equipments were procured
from the Kolkata based manufacturer to provide quality of power to withstand high
voltages. Whereas in North District where Sub-Stations are not overloaded, equipments
with ISI specification but relatively cheaper brand were quoted and hence the work for
each package was awarded at different ex-factory rates.

The reply is indicative of the fact that EPD failed to maintain a standardised procurement
policy and follow norms of economy and efficiency in execution of works. Moreover, the
variations in the rates of similar items in four districts were too significant which cannot
be justified by the reasons given by EPD.

Construction of temporary shed

2.3.3.13 EPD did not have its own Schedule of Rate (SOR) for Civil Construction
Works; hence it adopts the prevailing SOR of Roads & Bridges Department (RBD). The
SOR of RBD did not stipulate separate rate for construction of temporary sheds when
works are awarded on turnkey basis. The constructions of sub-stations and the alongside
temporary sheds for stacking of materials and also for accommodation of the chowkidar
for safe guarding of the materials etc. were integral parts of the turnkey contracts under
all eight packages (1 in East, 2 in West, 3 in North & 2 in South) of RGGVY projects.

Scrutiny of records, however, revealed that construction of temporary sheds was
incorporated in RGGVY Scheme as a separate item of works at extra costs, which
resulted in undue favour to the contactor to the tune of = 25.29 lakh as detailed in the
following table:



Table 2.3.7

Source: Letter of Awards

EPD stated (August 2013) that although construction of temporary sheds is included in
the estimates, no payment for the same had been made as on date. Reply confirms the fact
that EPD had committed against the liability towards construction of temporary sheds and
hence, payments against this item of work have to be made on submission of final claims
by the contractors.

awarded on turnkey basis to the contractors selected through open bid/tendering. During

were noticed which are enumerated below:

Non levy of Liquidated damages

2.3.3.15 EPD had awarded (February 2009) the works relating to RGGVY in four
districts to eight different contractors to be completed within 18 months upto August
2010. The terms of LoA provided for levy of liquidated damages on the defaulting
contractors/suppliers at the rate of 0.5 per cent per week of the value of material
supplied/erection works beyond schedule date subject to maximum of five per cent of
contract value.

Scrutiny of records revealed that supply of the materials was delayed by the suppliers to
the extent of 36 to 60 weeks. The EPD, however, failed to levy liquidated damages of
₹ 1.40 crore on the contractors as per the agreed terms and conditions.

EPD stated that due to frequent bandhs during 2008-10, inaccessibility of rural areas and
road blocks during monsoon due to landslides resulted in delay in delivery but there was
no cost over run in the projects. The reply, however, does not address the social costs
caused due to delays in the form of denial of the envisaged benefits of the project to
targeted BPL beneficiaries within the targeted period. Further, the inaccessibility and
landslides during monsoon is a common phenomenon in the State and the contractors
were aware of these conditions while entering into work agreement.

2.3.3.16 The work of supply and installation of BPL service connection from LT poles
to the consumers’ premises was taken up by the EPD departmentally. Issues noticed in
the departmental execution of works relating to BPL connections are discussed below:

Irregular purchase of BPL Kits

2.3.3.17 As per Rule 127 of Sikkim Financial Rules 1979, open tenders should be
invited in all purchases of stores costing more than = 10 lakh. During scrutiny of records,
it was revealed that EPD purchased the materials required for providing the connections
viz. electronic meter, wiring, GI pipe, MCB, cut out fuse, etc. valuing हैं 1.57 crore
directly from local firms in violation of the rules ibid.

EPD stated (January 2013) that the tendering procedure would not only be time
consuming but would also enhance the cost of the materials. The contention of EPD.
does not hold good the direct purchases made by EPD, which was contrary to REC
guidelines and Sikkim Financial Rules. Further, the claim of EPD regarding time
saving through direct purchases also proved wrong in view of the fact that the
electrification of BPL households has already been delayed and was still pending for
completion (September 2013).



Delay in electrification of BPL Households

2.3.3.18 As per the status of the scheme implementation as of November 2013, against
the target of electrifying 7,734!’ BPL Households in East (4,672'* Households) and South
Districts (3,062 Households) by August 2010, 6,059 BPL Households (East-3,294? and
South-2,765) could be electrified. Electricity connection to remaining 1,6757° BPL
households in East and South Districts could not be provided even after more than three
years of scheduled completion period (August 2010).

Audit analysed the reasons for delays in electrification of BPL houscholds in the East
District. It was noticed that after expiry (August 2010) of targeted date of electrification
of BPL households, GOS launched (September 2010) Chief Minister’s Rural Housing
Mission (CMRHM) for conversion of kucha houses covered for electrification under
RGGVY scheme into pucca houses. The works under CMRHM were, however, still in
progress (September 2013). This has in turn delayed electrification of the said BPL
households. The works of electrification of the above said 1378 BPL houscholds in East
District were, therefore, held up pending completion of CMRHM works.

It was observed that EPD had incurred an amount aggregating = 3.38 crore (March 2013)
in creating electrical infrastructure for electrification of the above BPL households in East
District. The expenditure incurred on RGGVY works, however, remained unfruitful in
absence of provision of electricity connections to BPL households due to taking up
(September 2010) of CMRHM works. Thus, failure of EPD to complete the RGGVY
works within the targeted period (August 2010), has caused blockage of fund to the extent
of 3.38 crore and due to this delay/failure the objective of electrifying BPL household in
time could also not be achieved.

EPD stated (August 2013) that electricity connections to the remaining households would
be provided by October 2013.

The fact, however, remains that targeted beneficiaries were deprived of the scheme
benefits despite creation of the required infrastructure.

2.3.3.19 With a view to augmenting implementation capacities for the programme,
REC had concluded Memorandum of Understanding (MOUs) with Central Public Sector
Undertakings”! (CPSUs) so as to make available the project management expertise and
capabilities of these organizations to the states wishing to use their services. As per REC
guidelines, the State Governments, if they so desire, could utilise the services of CPSUs
for implementation of the projects. Contrary to the suggestions made in the RGGVY

guidelines, however, EPD engaged consultants namely, M/s Mecon Limited for
implementation of RGGVY in the State. The following issues were noticed in this
regard:

Irregular selection of consultant

2.3.3.20 The Central Vigilance Commission’s (CVC) guidelines (July 2007) provide
that tendering process was a basic requirement for award of contract by any Government
agency as any other method, especially award of contract on nomination basis would
amount to breach of Article 14 of the Constitution guaranteeing right to equality to all
interested parties.

Scrutiny of records revealed that EPD appointed (February 2007) M/s Mecon Limited
(Mecon) as consultant to implement the RGGVY in the State without inviting the open
bids. The procedure adopted by the EPD was not transparent as allotment of consultancy
service was done arbitrarily without publicity and competition, jeopardising the principle
of equal opportunity. Further, the action of EPD had deprived the State of availing the
benefits of competitive bidding in terms of quality and cost of the scheme works.

As per the agreement entered (September 2007) between GoS and Mecon, duties of
Mecon, inter alia, included preparation of tender documents, scrutiny of technical and
commercial bids, assist GoS in issue of LoAs and preparation of contract agreements etc.
Consultancy fee payable to Mecon was 7 per cent of the total firmed up project cost. The
initial firmed up project cost of the four districts in the State was = 57.10 crore and as
such consultancy fee payable to Mecon was % 4 crore. The cost of RGGVY Project was
subsequently enhanced to = 148.91 crore (January 2009) based on the evaluation of bids
submitted by the lowest bidders. Although, the said revision did not involve increase in
the scope/quantum of work of the consultant, the consultancy fee of Mecon was revised
to ₹ 6.77 crore, which included fee of =2.77crore™ at the rate of 3.50 per cent on the
revised additional cost of the project. EPD could have avoided the payment of fee on the
enhanced project cost by incorporating suitable clause in consultancy agreement for
payment of additional amounts only based on the actual increase, if any, in the quantum.
of work. Failure to adhere to the tendering process and prudent commercial practices
resulted in excess payment to the extent of € 2.77 crore”.

EPD stated (August 2013) that the entire DPRs of RGGVY Scheme were based on the
Schedule of Rate 2003-04 which was prepared by the Engineers of the Department.
Mecon, a GOI Undertaking, was engaged so that the financial benefit accrued through
consultancy service would ultimately go into Government coffer.

The reply, however, does not address the issue of awarding the consultancy work by EPD
without inviting open tenders in violation of Sikkim Financial Rules, which deprived the
EPD from availing the best competitive price for the scheme works. Moreover as per the
agreement, EPD had to pay an additional amount of रैं 2.77 crore merely on account of
escalation in the project cost without involving any additional work/contribution by Mecon.

2. 270 crore x 3.5 per cent.

3.50 per cent of revised additional cost net of overheads {viz., 3.50 per cent of (हैं 91.81 crore-€ 12.67 crore)}.



Non adoption of REC’s guidelines on Mobilisation Advance

2.3.3.21 Clause 8.1 of Special Conditions of Contract (Volume IA) of the REC
guidelines provided that 15 per cent of the ex-works price of material components shall
be paid as initial advance in case of supply of materials for implementation of rural
electrification project. As regards the erection work contracts, the guidelines provided to
restrict the initial advance to 10 per cent of the total erection price. Contrary to these
guidelines, EPD paid interest free Mobilisation Advance (MA) to the extent of 25 per
cent on consolidated contract value of supply and erection works. Thus, the EPD had paid
excess mobilisation advance aggregating हैं. 14.92 crore to the turnkey contractors as
detailed in Appendix 2.3.3. This has caused consequent loss of interest amounting to
% 0.37" crore to EPD upto the period of adjustment of mobilisation advance against first
running bill submitted (September 2009) by the contractor.

EPD stated (August 2013) that the payment of 25 per cent MA was as per the prevalent
practice in the State. The reply confirms the fact that EPD failed in adherence to the REC
guidelines, which were mandatory for scheme implementation in the State.

Interest free Mobilisation Advance

2.3.3.22 CVC guidelines stipulated that the practice of extending interest free
Mobilisation Advance (MA) should be discouraged and that the recovery of the MA
should be affected in a time bound manner without linking it with the progress of works.
It was, however, observed that EPD extended (April 2009) interest free advances
aggregating हैं 33.36 crore to eight turnkey contractors before commencement of the
project and linked the recovery of the advance in proportion of the actual progress of
works in violation of the CVC guidelines. Since the EPD had been funding the said
interest free MA out of the REC loan bearing interest at the rate of five per cent, the EPD
had to sustain the interest loss on the said advances to the tune of € 1.83 crore”.

It was further noticed that out of = 33.36 crore released in April 2009, an amount of
₹ 1.99 crore remained unadjusted in respect of six contractors till date (September 2013).
The objective of releasing the MA was defeated as none of the projects was completed
within the time schedule.

During scrutiny of records, it was observed that the EPD had been charging interest at
five per cent per annum on the mobilisation advances granted to the contractor executing
other works. Audit is, therefore, of the view that the EPD should have levied the interest
at uniform rate on all advances including the advances granted for RGGVY works.
Further, in line with CVC guidelines the EPD should affect the recovery of the
mobilisation advance as per pre-determined time schedule without linking the same with
actual progress of works.

3... ₹ 14.92 crore x 5 per cent for 6 months (April to September 2009).
Worked out at the rate of 5 per cent per annum being interest charged by EPD on advance in respect of other works after netting

off the interest loss pointed out under Paragraph 3.2.3.2.1 supra



2.3.4.1 Under the scheme, 90 per cent capital subsidy would be provided by the GOI for
meeting overall cost of the projects. The balance project cost was to be met by the State
Government through loan from REC. Electrification of un-electrified BPL households
would, however, be financed through 100 per cent capital subsidy by GOI.

Funds received under the Scheme were to be kept in a separate interest bearing account.
EPD operated two current accounts, viz. Canara Bank and State Bank of Sikkim (SBS)
for management of funds received under the Scheme. Installments (both subsidy and loan
component) were released by REC by way of Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS)”®
transfer to the Canara Bank Account. The funds so deposited in Canara Bank were
transferred subsequently to the current account maintained with SBS and further
deposited under fixed deposit schemes. The following deficiencies were, however,
noticed in effective utilization of scheme funds by EPD:

Diversion of Scheme fund

2.3.4.2 The Scheme fund was required to be utilised for the purpose of creation of
infrastructure aimed at electrification of un-electrified villages and BPL houscholds.
During scrutiny of records, however, following instances of diversion of scheme funds
were noticed:

» EPD had utilised scheme funds to the extent of = 33.82 lakh for payment of interest
against loans availed from REC during the period from February 2009 to September
2009. This was contrary to the stipulations made in the Bipartite Agreement between.
REC and GOS according to which, the obligation for repayment of loan component
of scheme along with interest rests with the State Government.

v

Loan component of the Scheme was routed through the State Loan head by
depositing the loan amount into the State Government account instead of separate
RGGVY account. As a result, there were delays ranging from one month to five
months in receiving back the money (% 18.34 crore) from the State Loan head.

EPD stated (August 2013) that payment of interest on REC loan was made out of the
interest earned against scheme funds. EPD, however, accepted that there were delays in
receiving back the loan component after its transfer to Government account. The fact,

however, remains that there were no specific provisions under RGGVY guidelines for
utilising the scheme funds on above purposes.

Real Time Gross Settlement (RTGS)is a fund transfer mechanism where transfer of money takes place from one bank to another
on a “real time” and on “gross” basis. This is the fastest possible money transfer system through the banking channel.
Settlement in “real time” means payment transaction is not subjected to any waiting period. The transactions are settled as soon
as they are processed. "Gross settlement” means the transaction is settled on one to one basis without bunching with any other

transaction.



Idling of scheme funds

2.3.4.3 The REC guidelines provided that the funds received under the Scheme were to
be kept in a separate interest bearing accounts. It was noticed that out of the scheme funds
amounting to = 39.75 crore received (9 March 2012) by EPD, an amount of & 35 crore
was invested in Fixed Deposit only on 24 April 2012, i.e. after keeping the funds idle in
the current account for 45 days. This resulted in loss of interest income to the extent of
₹ 21.58 lakh”.

In reply, EPD admitted (August 2013) that the delay in investment of fund was due to
administrative delay and in the absence of expertise, an efficient fund management
system could not be established.

Booking of State Taxes

2.3.4.4 The MoP, GOI clarified (February 2008) that the State Taxes, like Value Added
Tax (VAT), Cess, etc. on RGGVY works would not be reimbursed and were to be borne
by the concerned State Government. Audit noticed that an amount of % 3.64 crore towards
VAT (₹ 2.78 crore) & Cess (% 0.86 crore) on RGGVY Scheme had been booked under
the RGGVY grant during April 2007 to March 2013.

It was observed that charging of State Taxes to the RGGVY was in violation of the ibid
instruction.

EPD stated (August 2013) that the guidelines of RGGVY Scheme do not interpret that the
State Taxes on RGGVY works had to be borne by the concerned State Government and
no notification/ circular was forwarded to EPD.

The reply is not valid as State duties and taxes were to be borne by the State Government
in view of ibid instructions (February 2008) of MoP, Gol.

Payment of freight and insurance charges without documentary proof

2.3.4.5 As per RGGVY Guidelines, insurance of materials while in transit and during
erection was mandatory in order to safeguard against incidence of unforeseen losses. In
terms of Letter of Award (LoAs) issued for implementing the RGGVY Scheme, the
turnkey contractors were entitled for reimbursement of freight and insurance charges
after submission of supporting documentary proof. It was, however, noticed that EPD
irregularly allowed the contractors reimbursement of insurance premium to the extent
%4.91 crore without obtaining the copies of insurance and freight charges documents in
violation of the RGGVY guidelines.

EPD stated (August 2013) that the insurance amount was based on the premium paid by
contractors. The EPD however, could not produce any documents to substantiate the reply.

Sper cent on रैं 35 crore for 45 days



2.3.5.1 The existence of an effective monitoring system is a pre-requisite for smooth
implementation of a scheme. It was, however, observed that due to inadequate MIS and
improper monitoring, there were instances of delays in decision making and
implementation of the scheme projects. The overall impact of the said delays on
implementation of the scheme remained largely un-assessed as discussed in the
subsequent paragraph.

State Level Coordination Committee

2.3.5.2 With a view to monitor the progress of implementation of RGGVY Scheme in
the State, the GoS constituted (September 2006) the State Level as well as District Level
Coordination Committees. As per the RGGVY guidelines, the State Level Committee
was required to conduct at least one meeting every month under the Chairmanship of the
Chief Secretary of the State to review the issues relating to the forest and environment
clearances, land acquisitions for sub-stations, etc. Similarly, District Level Committee,
headed by District Collector was also required to hold monthly review meetings to
monitor the progress of works. It was, however, observed in Audit that during the four
years period upto 2012-13 since the initiation of RGGVY Scheme (2009-10) in Sikkim
only 12 meetings were convened by these two Committees as against minimum number
of 96 meetings required to be convened as detailed below:

delay in taking important policy/major decisions to resolve bottlenecks in implementation
of RGGVY Scheme. Ineffectiveness of monitoring by State/District Level Coordination
Committees had consequently contributed towards delay in implementation of scheme in
the State.

EPD stated (August 2013) that meetings could not be held regularly due to the pre-

top-level monitoring was absent in implementation of the scheme.

Quality Monitoring Mechanism

2.3.5.3 The projects executed under the Scheme in the State were subject to a three-tier
Quality Monitoring Mechanism. The details of the Three Tier Quality Control
Mechanism prescribed in the Scheme are detailed in Appendix 2.3.4. The following
issues were noticed in the three-tier quality control mechanism.

Arbitrary selection of first tier Third Party Inspection Agency

2.3.5.4 The engagement of Third Party Inspection Agency (TPIA) for inspection of
ls was mandatory to ensure the execution of works as per norms and standards of
engineering. Further, to avail the benefits of most competitive price and quality of works,
the selection of TPIA should be made after inviting open tenders for the assignment. It
was observed that M/s Mecon Limited had offered (April 2009) to carry out the TPIA
service at one per cent of total project cost. EPD, however, arbitrarily appointed M/s
Biecco Lawrie Limited (BLL) for TPIA service of RGGVY Scheme at 2.5 per cent of
total project cost without following the open bidding process. The offer made by M/s
Mecon Limited was indicative of the availability of the firms willing to accept the work
assignment at fee lower than the fee charged by BLL. The EPD, however, could not
exploit the possibilities of fetching the economical rates from open market due to its
failure in appointing the TPIA through open tendering.

mate!

EPD stated (August 2013) that the offer of M/s Mecon Ltd. could not be accepted since
they were rendering the consultancy service in implementing the project and in order to
have independent monitoring report BLL was entrusted for TPIA service. The issue here,
however, is that open tendering should have been adopted for getting the best offers, as
required by the Sikkim Financial rules, which had not been done in this case.

Non-formulation of Quality Assurance Programme

2.3.5.5 As per the Quality Control Manual of REC (April 2008) EPD was to prepare a
detailed Quality Assurance Programme to ensure quality of work executed by the
Contractors and TPIA. All materials and villages were to be inspected as per the
Manufacturing Quality Plan and the Field Quality Plan. Even though the EPD had
engaged BLL as TPIA, no Quality Assurance Programme was prepared. Absence of a
proper and detailed Quality Assurance Programme was indicative of the fact that the
quality control mechanism was not implemented properly.

Improper inspection by TPIA

2.3.5.6 As per terms and conditions of the agreement, the TPIA was to carry out the
inspections in two stages i.e. on completion of infrastructure in 10 per cent villages (first
stage) and on completion of 90 per cent works in the villages (second stage). The first
stage of inspection was to be done in 10 per cent randomly selected villages. For second
stage inspection, 50 per cent villages were to be selected randomly including 10 per cent
villages inspected in the first stage. Further, the village wise inspection report was to be



submitted by TPIA on weekly basis to EPD for rectification.

Out of 227 villages inspected by TPIA, audit test checked the TPIA report in respect of
95° villages (42 per cent). The di
by TPIA were as follows:

pancies noticed on the inspection reports submitted

» The inspections were not done stage-wise as stipulated in the agreement. Instead
TPIA conducted the inspection at a stretch as per their convenience.

Vv

There were ten instances of conducting inspections of three or more villages by TPIA
in a single day indicating that inspections were carried out in a hurry by TPA.

Vv

As per the scope of the work, 10 per cent villages were to be inspected in first stage
and same villages were to be re-inspected during the second stage in order to ensure
rectification of defects pointed out in the first stage. Since the TPIA conducted the
inspection at a stretch, rectification of defects pointed out during the first stage
remained unverified. This led to defeating the very objective of third party inspection.

Vv

In 44 out of 123 villages under East District and in 26 out of 113 villages of West
District, the TPIA had reported “NO BPL FOUND”. The findings of TPIA were
contradictory to the figures of BPL connections (641) reported to have been provided
under the scheme as per the Monthly Progress Reports (MPR) submitted by EPD.
Further, as per the MPR, EPD had installed nine Distribution Transformers in nine
villages while the TPIA report indicated the same as ‘NIL’.

The above irregularities pointed out in the performance of TPIA are indicative of the fact
that TPIA did not adhere to the scope of work as laid down in the terms & conditions of
the agreement.

In reply, the EPD stated (August 2013) that three villages were inspected in a day, in
respect of villages located in the nearby area and there were insignificant irregularities.
The reply was, however, silent on other issues pointed out by Audit.

Second-tier quality control mechanism

2.3.5.7 The second tier quality control, inter alia, provided for pre-shipment inspection.
of at least one of the major materials at vendor’s works. The inspection of materials at
pre-shipment stage was stated to be carried out by REC. None of the Inspection Reports
issued by REC for the inspection carried out by them was, however, provided to Audit for
scrutiny.

Maintenance of the assets created under RGGVY

2.3.5.8 A substantial number of electrical infrastructures like 11 KV, LT lines and
installation of DITRs have been created under the scheme. The details of assets created
under the scheme are shown in Appendix 2.3.5.

* 65 villages in East and 30 villages in West District.



Proper maintenance of assets created plays an important role in reducing the risks
of breakdowns and accidents on account of mechanical failures. Taking a pro-active
stance towards maintenance of equipment is, therefore, crucial for achieving maximum
efficiency. It was, however, observed that although the LoA for the RGGVY works was
issued long back in February 2009, EPD had not earmarked any budgetary allocation for
the maintenance of scheme assets even after expiry of more than four years of
commencement of scheme implementation (2008-09) till March 2013. The photographs
below are the apparent examples of the poor maintenance of the assets created in the
State under the RGGVY scheme:

While accepting facts, the EPD stated (August 2013) that budget provisions for
maintenance would be created after completion of RGGVY works. It is. however,
observed that the assets created under the scheme required regular maintenance
considering their continuous utilisation for scheme implementation. Inadequate and
ineffective maintenance of these assets could lead to deterioration in their value and
capacities.

Separate records of the assets created under RGGVY

2.3.5.9 As per the Bipartite Agreement between REC and GoS (August 2005), the State
Government shall be the owner of the assets created under the scheme. During scrutiny of
the records of East and West districts, it was noticed that no separate records had been
maintained by EPD for the assets created under the scheme. In absence of separate
records for the scheme assets, the physical and financial details of these assets could not
be verified in Audit.

Awareness programme under RGGVY

2.3.6.1 Awareness programme plays a major role in creating awareness among BPL
beneficiaries about the RGGVY Scheme and its objective. EPD had not conducted any
awareness programme at the district/village level. No signboards indicating various



details on implementation of the scheme (such as, the broad objectives and envisaged
benefits of scheme, area and population covered, timeline for completing scheme works,
cost of the works involved, etc.) were placed at any prominent places like panchyat
office, schools, etc. of the villages where the scheme was implemented. During
beneficiary survey of 100 BPL households conducted by Audit in 20 villages of 2
Districts, it was observed that none of the BPL Households had the knowledge about the
RGGVY scheme and their entitlements under the scheme.

The EPD stated (August 2013) that awareness programmes were conducted by the
panchayats. No documentary or other evidence was, however produced by EPD in
support of their claim. Further, the results of the survey carried out by Audit on randomly
selected 100 BPL households for assessing the knowledge about the scheme points to
absence of awareness programme.

Non provision of earthing

2.3.6.2. The G.I. earthing spike with nuts was an integral package of one point free
connection service to the BPL households to ensure safety of the beneficiaries. During
the field survey of 50 Beneficiaries in 10 villages in West district, it was observed that
earthing was not provided in any of the BPL Houscholds connections surveyed by Audit.

Non provision of meters

2.3.6.3 The EPD had to provide meters in all rural households. Without installation of
meter, the meter readings could not be taken and the revenue sustainability as conceived
under the scheme could not be ensured. It was, however, observed that no meter was
provided in the premises of any of the five’! beneficiaries surveyed under Chisopani
Village in Gangtok Block and in four out of five households surveyed in Umchung
Village, Gyalshing Block.

Distribution Transformers

2.3.6.6 As per the LoAs issued for award of project works, all the Distribution

2.3.6.7 As per specification of REC, galvanized material was to be used for construction.
of distribution sub-stations. During physical verification by Audit Team in Pachak
