Provision of electricity to public places

2.3.6.8 The scheme aimed at providing electricity to public places like schools,
panchayat offices, health centres, dispensaries, community centres, etc. During the survey
in selected 20 villages, it was noticed that all the primary health centres and the panchayat
offices in these villages were provided with electrical connections. It was, however,
noticed that three schools in three villages, viz. Soreng Primary School, MendoGaon
Primary School and Yangtam Sanskrit Secondary School were deprived of the electricity
due to improper survey and non-inclusion of these schools in DPR. As per the RGGVY
Guidelines the villages cannot be considered as electrified in absence of proper
electrification of schools.



It was stated during the survey by the Engineers that the schools concerned had not
applied for the connections. EPD needs to look into the reasons for not covering these
schools under the scheme and take appropriate action for their electrification. Further,
EPD also needs to ensure that no such cases exist in other villages of the State by
obtaining confirmation from the Gram panchayats of each electrified village covered
under the scheme and also through conducting the survey/physical inspection of the
villages covered under the scheme, on random basis.

2.3.7.1 We acknowledge the cooperation and assistance extended by different levels of
officers and staff of the EPD at various stages of conducting Audit.

2.3.8.1 The objective of RGGVY was to provide access to electricity to all rural
households and improving the rural electricity distribution infrastructure by March 2009.
The RGGVY scheme could not be completed in its entirety even after four and a half
years of scheduled completion time due to various deficiencies in its implementation. It
was seen that the EPD failed to prepare a Rural Electrification Plan which was to be a
roadmap for rural electrification. This led to cascading delay in all subsequent
implementation of RGGVY Scheme right from creation of RGGVY infrastructure and
granting free connection to the BPL beneficiaries. The Detailed Project Reports prepared
for scheme project were deficient in various aspects leading to subsequent revisions in
work scope and consequent time and cost overrun. Instances of delays in award of work,
acquisition of land and submission of revised quantity estimates etc. were also noticed
which prolonged completion of the Scheme. Cost of implementation of the Scheme
exceeded the cost norms due to significant variations in the ex-works price for
equipments of same specification. There were diversions of Scheme funds, irregular
inclusion of State taxes in the Scheme cost and inadmissible payments to consultants/
turnkey contractors. Despite this delay in execution of scheme works, regular meetings of
State level/ District level Coordination Committees were not convened. Beneficiary
surveys conducted by Audit also revealed instances of several shortcomings in execution
of scheme works, which remained un-rectified in absence of adequate evaluation of
works completed by EPD and prompt corrective action against the shortcomings noticed.

2.3.9.1 It is recommended that:

> State Plans which are integral to implementation of Central Schemes should be
finalised on time to achieve the objectives of the Schemes more effectively and
efficiently.

> Delay in implementation of Schemes could better be avoided through prompt

award of work and advance action with respect to land acquisition and other



preparatory activities. The DPRs for the project should be realistic and based on
the authentic data of the beneficiaries

> Economy should be maintained in execution of works and scheme funds should be
utilised in strict compliance with the scheme guidelines.
> Progress of works should be effectively monitored through monitoring

committees and prompt corrective action should be taken against short comings
noticed for successful implementation of the scheme.

Concerned with the slow growth in the Agriculture and allied sectors, the National
Development Council (NDC), in its meeting held on 29 May 2007, resolved to launch a
special Additional Central Assistance Scheme Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RKVY) to
incentivise States to draw up plans for their agriculture sector more comprehensively.
This would involve a new scheme for Additional Central Assistance to State Plans,
administered by the Union Ministry of Agriculture over and above its existing Centrally
Sponsored Schemes, to supplement the State-specific strategies including special schemes
for beneficiaries of land reforms. Thus, RKVY aimed at achieving four per cent annual
growth in the Agriculture sector during the XI Plan period, by ensuring a holistic
development of Agriculture and allied sectors. The main objectives of the scheme inter
alia were:

(i) to incentivise the States so as to increase public investment in Agriculture and allied
sectors; (ii) to achieve the goal of reducing the yield gaps in important crops, through
focused interventions; (iii) to bring about quantifiable changes in the production and
productivity of various components of Agriculture and allied sectors by addressing them
in a holistic manner.

In Sikkim, the Agriculture and allied sector had achieved four per cent annual growth
rate towards contribution to Gross State Domestic Product (GSDP) during the XI Plan
period. However, in cases of production of major crops (i.e. Cereals, Pulses and Oil
seeds), the four per cent growth rate was achieved only in two years (2008-09 and
2011-12 for Cereals and Pulses and 2009-10 and 2011-12 for Oil seeds) of the period
under review.

Adequate and envisaged planning process for overall vision for development of the
district was absent both in the District Agriculture Plan (DAP) and State Agriculture
Plan (SAP) and it was compounded with flaws in the preparation of DPRs. As a result,
the gaps in various agricultural initiatives remained unidentified and the improvement in
the production of agricultural products was also not achieved. Even the financial targets
set by the Department were unrealistic and highly inflated.



The smooth functioning of the scheme and achievement of its objectives was adversely
affected by improper fund management, delays, taking up of unviable projects and lack of
monitoring, review, evaluation and field studies from the State Level Selection Committee

(SLSC).

|

(Paragraph 2.4.9.3)

(Paragraph 2.4.13.1)

(Paragraph 2.4.14.5)

Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojana (RK VY) was launched with the aim of achieving four per
cent annual growth in the Agricultural sector during the XI Plan period by ensuring
holistic development of Agriculture and allied sectors with the objectives to incentivise
the State to invest on Agriculture sector, maximise profits to the farmers, bring about
quantifiable change in the production ete.

The funds under the scheme were provided to States as 100 per cent grant by the Central
Government. The eligibility for assistance under the scheme would depend upon the
amount provided in State Plan Budget for Agriculture and allied sectors, over and above
the base line percentage expenditure™ incurred by the State Governments on Agriculture
and allied sectors. The State would ensure that the baseline share of agriculture in its total
State Plan expenditure (excluding the assistance under the RKVY) is at least maintained,
and upon its doing so, it would be able to access the RKVY funds.

The RKVY would be available to the States in two distinct streams i.e. at least 75 per
cent of the allocated amount under Stream I for specific projects and the balance amount
would be available under Stream II for strengthening the existing State sector schemes
and filling the resource gaps. A State Level Sanctioning Committee (SLSC), constituted,
would have the authority to sanction specific projects under Stream I.

* Denotes the average percentage of expenditure during the previous three years.



The Food Security and Agriculture Development Department was the Nodal Department
for the implementation of the scheme. The Department was headed by the Secretary and
assisted by the Principal Director, Director and other Officers. The SLSC was constituted
(December 2007) for sanctioning of the projects at State level and to review the
implementation of the scheme and to ensure that the projects were implemented in
accordance with the guidelines laid down by the Central Government. The schemes were
being implemented by three“ departments in addition to the Food Security and
Agricultural Development Department (Nodal Department).

The audit covered the period from 2007-08 to 2012-13. Audit examined the records and
evidence collected from the Secretariat as well as the four district offices of the
departments of Food Security and Agriculture Development Department (FSADD),
Animal Husbandry, Livestock, Fisheries and Veterinary Services Department (AHLFVS),
Horticulture and Cash Crop Development Department (HCCD) and Co-operation
Department. The audit was conducted during June to August 2013.

The audit of RKVY was taken up with the objectives of assessing whether:

» Planning process of the implementation of scheme was effective and in compliance
with the RKVY guidelines;

v

Financial management ensured adequate and timely availability of funds and their
effective and economic utilisation;

» Projects were implemented according to the regulatory structure in place and the
intended objectives of the projects were achieved and the Nodal agency had
effectively coordinated with various departments and implementing agencies for
implementing various projects;

» Internal control mechanisms were put in place to ensure efficient and effective
monitoring and control over implementation; monitoring mechanism at each level
was adequate; and

Vv

The objectives of maximising returns to the farmers in Agriculture and allied sectors
were achieved and the State could achieve four per cent growth in the agricultural
sector during the XI Plan period.

Animal Hushandry, Livestock, Fisheries and Veterinary Services Department, Horticulture and Cash Crop Development
Department and Co-operation Department.

> Guidelines for RKVY of Department of Agriculture & Cooperation, Ministry of

The audit for the period 2007-08 to 2012-13 commenced with an entry conference with
the Secretaries, other Officers of the Nodal Department as well as the implementing
departments in July 2013. Records maintained at the departmental headquarters and four
districts were test checked during the Audit. The exit conference was held in October
2013.

From the consolidated list of 78 projects, 18 from Stream I were selected using
Probability Proportional to Size With Replacement (PPSWR) method. Two projects were
selected from Stream TI. All the four districts were selected.

24.7.1

Preparation and submission of District Agriculture Plan (DAP)/State
Agriculture Plan (SAP).

According to the guidelines of RKVY, each district would formulate DAP by including
the resources available under various existing schemes (i.e. District, State, or Central
schemes, etc.) and adhere to the guidelines circulated by the Planning Commission for
District Planning. The DAP would present the vision for Agriculture and allied sectors
within the overall development perspective of the district and would present the financial
requirement and the sources of financing for the agriculture development plans in a
comprehensive way. The objective of district planning was to design an integrated and
participatory action plan for the development of the local area in general and Agriculture



and allied sectors in particular. For projecting the actual requirements for development of
Agriculture and allied sectors of the district, the planning process was to be initiated from
the grass root level ie. at village/Gram Panchayat Level. The Nodal Department was to
ensure that the State’s priorities with respect to Agriculture and allied sectors are
appropriately captured in the DAPs. After preparation of the DAPs, the Nodal
Department would finalise the comprehensive SAP by integrating all the DAPs. After the
approval of SAP, the Nodal Department would place the Stream I proposals before the
SLSC for consideration, discussion, finalisation, and sanction.

Scrutiny of DAPs and SAP revealed that there was no reference to the on-going
schemes/programmes (other than the RKVY projects) in the SAP and DAPs and that
merely the names of other on-going schemes (Appendix 2.4.1) were provided in the
DAPs. Scrutiny of the SAP and DAPs also indicated that the Department had not taken
any step to explore and identify the possibility of convergence of RKVY with other
existing schemes/projects. Hence, the DAPs and SAP could not adequately integrate
resources available from different sources with RKVY which was an objective of the
scheme. The shelf of projects at district level was not prepared. Documents in support of
the initiation of the planning process from village/GP level though called for, was not
produced to Audit. The DAPs were prepared without considering the overall resources
available for development of Agriculture and allied sectors and also without any inputs
from the village/GP level. The DAPs and SAP appeared to be mainly an exercise to
obtain funds from GOI.

Records regarding the submission of SAP and DAPs to the DAC and Development
Planning, Economic Reforms & North Eastern Council Affairs Department
(DPERNECAD) of State Government were not produced to Audit.

Thus, adequate and well-envisaged planning process for overall vision for development of
the district was absent in the DAPs. The flaws in the preparation of SAP and DAPs
resulted in non-viable projects being taken up, irregular implementation of projects and
non- improvement in agricultural production, as elaborated in the succeeding paragraphs.
2.4.7.2 Detailed Project Report

Detailed Project Report (DPR) is a basic document for planning and implementing a
project. The DPR is to be prepared carefully and with sufficient details of the project to
ensure its appraisal and implementation in an economical and efficient manner. In a well-
prepared project, all the essential ingredients would be properly considered and included,
i.e. feasibility studies, competencies of the implementing agencies, anticipated benefits to
the farmers, definite time-lines for implementation, etc. The Nodal Agency would satisfy
itself that the project fulfilled the objectives of the RKVY before recommending the
DPRs to the SLSC.

Audit observed that the DPRs were prepared by the implementing departments without
proper surveys and investigations and lacked the essential inputs. The Nodal Department
had submitted these DPRs to the SLSC without proper verification. Details of
shortcomings in the DPRs are given below:



> Project on Baby Corn, Sweet Corn and Pop corn:
The DPR was prepared taking into consideration only the input cost and without any
market survey. The selling price, earning and availability of market, etc. were not
spelt out in the DPR. This resulted in the implementation of a non-viable project as
detailed in Paragraph 2.4.10.1.
> Establishment of Feed unit at Melli Payong:

The SLSC approved the establishment of Feed unit at Melli Payong at a total cost of
% 1.21 crore scheduled to be completed by 31 March 2013. The project was being
implemented by Food Security and Agriculture Development Department (FSADD).
Scrutiny of records revealed that the DPR only stated the financial aspect of the
project and did not quantify the benefit that was to be delivered to the farmers. The
current market price of feed and the selling price of feed from proposed unit were not
taken into consideration for calculating detailed benefits (cost benefit ratio) that was
to be delivered to the farmers. Moreover, the feasibility study was also not
conducted. Due to the inherent defects in the DPR, the project was not taken up even
after two years of sanction. Even after inviting the offer by the Department for
construction and operation of the project, only two agencies responded to the bids,
one of which offered very low rate and another offered bids without any supporting
documents and rate. After obtaining the approval, the Department again appointed a
firm for preparation of Project Report at a total cost of = 6 lakh which indicated that
the initial DPR was prepared without proper scrutiny by the Nodal Department.

> Packing house for fruits and Flowers:
The DPR had detailed the expenditure on the construction of the infrastructure only,
that too, without taking into account the requirement of a packing house. The
benefits that were to be delivered to the farmers and its impact on agricultural
production were not specified in the DPR.

> Establishment of Piggery farm at Melli Dara:
Establishment of Piggery farm at Melli Dara at a total cost of = 90.67 lakh was
approved by SLSC in October 2009. The project was being implemented by
AHLFVSD. The work was awarded to the contractor at par to be completed within
June 2010. After the payment (October 2010) of the first running bill, the scope of
work was changed (February 2011) and a new estimate was framed (April 2011) for
% 1.19 crore. This validated the fact that the initial DPR was prepared without proper
survey and investigation. Due to the improper preparation of the DPR, the project
remained incomplete even after a lapse of more than three years from the date of
approval by SLSC.

The Department while accepting (November 2013) the audit contention stated that the
estimate had to be revised due to non-inclusion of provision for the water supply,
approach road, disposal pit/ slurry etc. in the original estimate.

2.4.8.2 Short release of funds

During 2008-09, the GOI released only हैं 5.68 crore against the approved amount of
% 11.37 crore and balance amount of % 5.69 crore was adjusted from the allocation of
2010-11. Due to this, the State Government failed to take up new schemes for हैं 5.69
crore during 2010-11 and projects for the year 2008-09 were delayed for want of funds.
During 2011-12, against the approved amount of % 31.84 crore, only = 24.64 crore was
released leaving a balance amount of % 7.20 crore. However, the projects taken up during
2011-12 were completed by utilising funds from the normal allocation of 2012-13. Due to
this, the State Government failed to take up new schemes for the amount of € 7.20 crore
during 2012-13.

During 2011-12, against release of = 24.64 crore by GOI (% 20.08 crore was received
during 2011-12 and % 4.56 crore was received during 2012-13), utilisation certificates for



only हैं 9.01 crore was submitted till September 2012 by the Nodal Department. Due to the
non-submission of the UCs pertaining to release of funds during 2011-12, the GOI
released only हैं 15.21 crore during 2012-13 out of the total approved funds of हैं 29.47
crore and hence the State lost = 14.26 crore.

Thus, the State lost a total amount of = 27.15 during the years 2008-13 which could have
benefitted many needy people.

2.4.8.3 Expenditure incurred out of one per cent share of RKVY funds

A State is permitted to use upto one per cent of its total RKVY funds for incurring
administrative expenditure that includes payments to consultants, recurring expenses of
various kinds, staff costs, etc. In the State of Sikkim, one per cent of its total RKVY
funds for incurring administrative expenditure were kept for 2009-10 only. During
2009-10, = 15.29 lakh was allocated for administrative expenditure of which only हैं 2.37
lakh was spent during the year and out of the unspent balance of € 12.92 lakh, only हैं 9.87
lakh could be spent during 2010-11. During 2011-12, against the provision of हैं 13.15
lakh as administrative expenditure, हैं. 16.26 lakh was incurred which resulted in an excess
expenditure of हैं 3.11 lakh. During 2012-13, = 15.68 lakh was spent on administrative
expenses out of which, हैं 8.26 lakh was spent on inadmissible items like discretionary
grant, petrol, oil and lubricants for the Chairperson of the FSADD and purchase and
distribution of water harvesting tanks beyond the scope of RKVY.

2.4.9.1 Delay in implementation of projects

Out of the 20 selected projects, eight projects (estimated cost हैं 12.46 crore) with actual
expenditure of % 5.95 crore scheduled to be completed by March 2013 remained
incomplete. This not only resulted in blockage of funds but also deprived the beneficiaries
of the intended benefits. Details of some of the projects which were delayed are given
below:

2.4.9.1.1 Assistance to Sikkim Organic Mission

Assistance to the Sikkim Organic Mission (SOM) was approved by SLSC (June 2011) at
a total cost of हैं 2.50 crore with the objective of promoting organic farming by
construction of vermi compost and rural compost pits. Funds amounting to = 2.50 crore
were transferred to SOM during February 2012 (₹ 1.25 crore) and March 2012 (@ 1.25
crore). हैं 1.25 crore was kept in savings bank accounts and हैं 1.25 crore was advanced to
Sikkim State Cooperative Supply and Marketing Federation Ltd. (SIMFED) by SOM for
construction of vermi compost and rural compost pits. As of August 2013, work was not
completed and advance was lying unadjusted. However, the utilisation certificate
submitted to the Nodal Department reported that the sum of % 1.25 crore advanced to
SIMFED had been utilised for the purpose for which it had been sanctioned. Reasons for
non- completion of the project were not found on records.



2.4.9.1.2 Construction of godown for multipurpose Co-operative Society, Luing
Perbing

Construction of godown for multipurpose Co-operative Society at Luing Perbing was
approved (June 2011) by the SLSC at a total cost of = 30 lakh with the objective of
catering to the post harvest requirement of production and marketable surplus of various
farm products. The work was undertaken by the Society for completion by 31 March
2013. The Nodal Department released = 15 lakh (October 2012) against the project. This
was expended by the Society for construction of godown. However, as of August 2013,
the work remained incomplete. The reason for delay in completion was not on records.

2.4.9.1.3 Establishment of Piggery farm

Establishment of Piggery farm at a total cost of हैं 90.67 lakh was approved (October
2009) by SLSC. The work was awarded to the contractor at par with estimated cost with
the stipulation for completion by June 2010. However, even after incurring an
expenditure of हैं 74.66 lakh, the project remained incomplete till the date of Audit
(August 2013). The reason for non-completion of the project was frequent changes in the
scope of work.

The Department stated (November 2013) that the civil work of the farm was now
completed and going to be inaugurated in December 2013.

2.4.9.2 Non-convergence of RKVY with other existing schemes/projects

A SLSC was constituted in December 2007 under RKVY which was responsible for
sanctioning the projects of the scheme and to ensure that no duplication of efforts or
resources took place. GOI also advised (February 2009) the State Government to ensure
that there was no duplication/overlapping of activities/areas covered under RKVY
projects vis-a-vis existing schemes of Central or State Governments. During scrutiny of
records, it was observed that contrary to the above instructions, similar type of projects
such as construction of water harvesting tank with a capacity of 10,000 litres was taken
up by the FSADD under Macro Management of Agriculture (MMA).

The FSADD being the monitoring authority for MMA and HCCDD being a member of
SLSC had failed to prevent the overlapping of the projects. This was also the result of
improper preparation of DAPs and SAP by not including all the Central and State
schemes for development of Agriculture in the State.

2.4.9.3 Excess expenditure

The SLSC approved (November 2008) the proposal for the ‘Creation of Irrigation
facilities for efficient water management and roof water harvesting’ under Stream-I at a
total cost of = 4.50 crore. The beneficiaries were entitled to financial assistance of 50
per cent as subsidy for construction of 10,000 litres capacity Roof Water Harvesting RCC
tanks.

The Detailed Project Report was prepared by the Engineering Cell for construction of
1,384 tanks at an estimated cost of हैं 65,380 per tank (on the basis of SOR 2006) and the



project was implemented during 2008-09. The beneficiaries were given 50 per cent of the
cost as subsidy, which amounted to % 32,500.

Scrutiny of records revealed that during 2009-10, under the scheme MMA, the
Department constructed (2009-10) similar rain water harvesting tanks with equivalent
capacity for which the estimated cost was = 54,520 per tank prepared on the basis of SOR
2006. Although the project for providing the tanks under RKVY was earlier than that
under the MMA scheme, it was found that the Department had estimated a higher cost for
the construction of water harvesting tanks under RKVY by non-deduction of contractor’s
profit and cost escalation of 20 per cent as against the cost escalation of 15 per cent
provided in the MMA scheme. Thus, the estimate of RKVY was inflated by 10,860 per
tank. Scrutiny further revealed that in actual execution of project under RKVY, there was
an excess expenditure of % 7,500°° per tank as compared to the construction of tank
under MMA, which resulted in excess expenditure of = 1.04 crore for the entire scheme.

Joint physical verification of 56 tanks (East-22, South-10, West-14 and North-10) out of
1,384 tanks, conducted with the departmental officers in four districts revealed that:

> 15 tanks (East-4, South-1, West-3 and North-7) remained defunct due to leakage of
water ;

In all 56 cases, the beneficiaries’ contribution was less than the estimated amount of
& 32,500 per tank; and

were found.

2.4.10.1 Cultivation of baby corn and sweet corn

The Government of Sikkim, introduced cultivation of baby corn and sweet corn in Sikkim
for the sustainable development of farmers. The two projects had been sanctioned at a
total cost of हैं 0.63 crore and हैं 1.52 crore under “Other sector” and “Crops sector” of
RKVY during 2011-12 and 2012-13 respectively. Similarly, under the Chief Minister's
Package of State Plan, same types of projects were implemented during 2011-12 and
2012-13, but the Department had not segregated the production scheme-wise. Hence, the
total expenditure of the Chief Minister’s Package as well as RKVY was taken into
account to arrive at input cost. The Department also had not segregated the sales value
scheme-wise. The Department had incurred an expenditure of हैं 3.59°° crore for the
procurement and supply of baby corn, sweet corn and manure to the farmers during the
year 2011-12 and 2012-13. The procurement and marketing of the corn was entrusted to
SIMFED with the stipulation that हैं. 10 per kg would be paid to SIMFED as market
support price.

However, during the year 2011-12 and 2012-13, the expenditure on inputs for producing

in input cost exceeding the amount obtained from sales by हैं 2.49 crore. Hence, income
from the agricultural output of baby corn and sweet corn was less than the expenditure
incurred for its production. It was observed that the DPR was prepared by taking into
consideration only the input cost and without any market survey. The selling price,
earning potential, availability of market, and viability of the projects were not spelt out in
the DPR.

It was also noticed that SIMFED sustained a loss of = 15.40 lakh in the business of corn
production. For the sustainable development of the farmers, the Department provided all
required items to the beneficiaries, but in absence of proper market research, the
Department ended up taking economically unviable projects.

2.4.10.2 Project of Ginger processing

The State Cabinet sanctioned (December 2008) a proposal of the HCCDD for the
establishment of a Ginger Processing Unit at Birdang, West Sikkim, at an estimated cost
of हैं 3,057 crore for manufacturing clean ginger, ginger powder, ginger oil and ginger
paste from fresh ginger with the objectives of providing value addition to the ginger crop
grown in the State and thereby ensuring better prices for the farmers involved in ginger
farming.

Pre-commissioning of the Unit was done by the Department in June 2010 through trial

runs and the infrastructure was handed over to SIMFED in January 2011 on lease at an
annual rent of € 16.60 lakh on the basis of bids (October 2010).

Afier taking over the Unit and subsequent trial runs, the SIMFED reported (August 2011)
that the manufacturing unit was mainly designed for production of ginger oil and was not
in a position to produce other allied products like ginger paste, ginger powder etc. Due to
the low recovery percentage of ginger oil, the operation of the unit was found
economically non-viable. The Unit therefore required additional machinery and
equipment for the production of other allied product like ginger powder, ginger paste etc.

The HCCDD thereafter spent 36.73 lakh (March 2012) on the project (% 21.84 lakh on
machinery and equipment and % 14.89 lakh on electrification, water supply and fitting &
fixing of industrial tiles) by diverting funds earmarked for CM’s package for dry and
backward areas (है. 21.84 lakh) and Construction of Horticulture Inspectors Centre
(₹ 14.89 lakh).

A team of officers from the SIMFED and HCCDD inspected the unit in May 2012 and
reported that due to various defects and shortcomings™*, it was unable to process raw

2.4.11.1 Water Management for orchard

Project on Water Management for orchard with the objective of increasing orange
production for generating higher returns to the farmers was submitted by HCCDD and
was approved (June 2011) by the SLSC at a total cost of € 1 crore for construction of 16
number of fabricated water storage tanks. Accordingly, 16 tanks were constructed at a
total cost of € 95 lakh.

Scrutiny of records revealed that in three cases in the East district, the project was not
implemented to increase orange production as stipulated in the DPR, but it was
constructed for the cultivation of vegetables. This was corroborated by the joint physical
verification.

During the joint physical verification (August-September 2013) of seven tanks with the
departmental officers, it was noticed that the water of six tanks was not utilised for orange
cultivation, instead it was used mostly for cultivation of vegetables and household
purposes. Discussion with the User Committees during joint physical verification
revealed that orange cultivation was not viable in the area.

In one case (Salleybong, South Sikkim), only one beneficiary utilised the water of tank
for household purposes but the pipes meant for connection from water source were lying
idle (as shown in the photograph). Instead of using the pipes for the procured purpose the
existing old pipeline was used in its place. In another case, a tank at Kamrang South
District was lying empty, unused, and the pipeline from the water tank connecting to the
field of the beneficiaries were disjointed.

In one case in East district, four connections were provided, but only three were in
operation as shown in the photographs below. According to the Secretary of Water Users
Committee, only seven persons benefited from the tanks which was less than what was
stipulated (20 beneficiaries) in the DPR.



The above facts indicated that the Department constructed the tanks without assessing the
actual requirement for orange cultivation and proper survey and investigation. This led to
utilisation of RKVY funds for unapproved items of works and deprival of the benefit to
the intended beneficiaries.

2.4.11.2 Trout fish farming

The project ‘Trout fish farming’ at a total cost of ¢ 77 lakh was approved by the SLSC
during August 2010. The project was implemented by the Directorate of Fisheries under
the AHLFVSD. The project included construction of raceways and distribution of feeds
and seeds. The unit cost of the project for each beneficiary was हैं 2.30 lakh, out of which
50 per cent subsidy was provided by the Government which amounted to हैं 1.15 lakh
(construction of raceway- हैं 50,000, cost of feed - = 50,000 and cost of seed - हैं 15,000).
The project was dovetailed with National Fisheries Development Board (NFDB). The
estimate of the project was framed without preparing the DPR.

Total of 106 beneficiaries were identified and the total project cost amounted to € 126.90
lakh, out of which हैं 77 lakh was released by the Nodal Department under RKVY and the
remaining amount of € 49.90 lakh was funded by NFDB.

Test check of the records of the Directorate of Fisheries revealed the following:

> The Nodal Department released = 77 lakh in two equal instalments during March
2011 and January 2012. The first instalment pertained to subsidy of हैं 50,000 per
beneficiary for cost of construction of raceways and the second instalment pertained
to cost of feed and seed of % 65,000 per beneficiary. Audit scrutiny revealed that the



construction of the raceways was completed by January 2012 whereas the Directorate
of Fisheries distributed the subsidised cost of seeds of € 15,000 only in May 2013.
This led to non-utilisation of raceways and undue delay in the implementation of the
project.

The Directorate stated (November 2013) that due to the shortage of seed, it had to
distribute the same partially during 2012 and the balance during April 2013. The reply
was indicative of improper planning in the Directorate.

> No guideline was formulated for the implementation of ‘Trout fish farming’ under
RKVY. However, since the project was dovetailed with NFDB, the guidelines of
NFDB have been followed. As per the said guidelines, the subsidy to be provided was
20 per cent to general farmers and 25 per cent to SC/ST farmers, limited to हैं 46,000
for general farmers and हैं 57,500 for SC/ST farmers. However, in actual execution,
the subsidy provided was 50 per cent of the total cost (i.e. = 1,15,000). This led to an
unauthorised expenditure of = 69,000 per general farmer and = 58,000 per SC/ST
farmer.

The Directorate stated (November 2013) that the approval from the State Government
was obtained to provide 50 per cent subsidy. The reply was not acceptable as the
scheme guidelines were violated.

> Out of 106 raceways (30-East, 20-North, 20-South, 36-West), earthquake of
September 2011 had damaged 27 (03-East, 10-North, 09-South, 05-West), which
were still lying defunct as of August-September 2013. No action had been initiated by
the Directorate or by beneficiaries to restore the damaged raceways. Thus, non-
restoration of the raceways either by the Department or by the beneficiaries resulted
in unfruitful expenditure of = 23.61 lakh. Further, the Directorate unjustifiably issued
(January and May 2013) seed and feed to the beneficiaries, even though their
taceways were damaged.

The Directorate stated (November 2013) that State Government had been requested
to provide assistance to the beneficiaries for restoration of the raceways and as soon



v

v

Vv

as the funds were received from the Government, the raceways would be restored.
The fact remained that as of November 2013, the raceways remained defunct.

During May 2012, the Directorate of Fisheries procured 49.98 tonnes of feed for three
districts (East - 21.42 tonnes, North and South - 14.28 tonnes each) through SIMFED.
for distribution among 70 beneficiaries (East-30, North and South-20 each) @ 714
kgs per beneficiary. However, only 19.43 tonnes (East-9.8 tonnes, North-9 tonnes and
South- 8.43 tonnes) of feed were distributed to the beneficiaries and 30.55 tonnes
worth हैं 21.35 lakh were lying undistributed in the departmental Stores. In case of
West district, feed had not been distributed to 6 beneficiaries out of 36 due to
incomplete construction of raceways which were lying idle (September 2013). Non-
distribution of feed led to deprival of intended benefits to the beneficiaries. The
reasons for non-distribution of feed to the beneficiaries, except West district, were not
found in the records of the Directorate.

The Directorate stated (November 2013) that the feed was being distributed in phased
manner due to the shortage of proper storage facilities of the beneficiaries.

The Department had not conducted any assessment regarding production of trout fish.
The actual production of trout fish after implementation of the scheme was also not on
records of the Directorate. In the absence of which, Audit could not ascertain the
success of the project.

While accepting the audit observation, the Directorate stated (November 2013) that
the assessment will be conducted and intimated to Audit.
The DPR was also not prepared. The project was implemented on the basis of
guidelines of NFDB. Further, the vouchers regarding the purchase of feed from
SIMFED, though called for, were not produced to Audit.

The Directorate stated (November 2013) that detailed programme estimate and input
cost per unit was prepared and approval was sought for accordingly. The reply was
not acceptable as the Directorate has not prepared any DPR as required under the
RKVY guidelines.


2.4.11.3 Abandonment of Project

Documentation on farm conservation of native and underutilised crops was approved
(October 2009) by SLSC at a total project cost of हैं 0.57 crore. The objectives of the
project were documentation of local cultivars”, facilitation for germ plasma registration
for the farmers, on-farm seed production of local superior cultivars, identification of
specific local superior cultivars, facilitation of on-farm conservation and cultivation of
local cultivars, livelihood improvement through local production and capacity building of
farmers. The scheme was to be implemented by FSADD through the Indian Council of
Agricultural Resource. However, after incurring an expenditure of हैं 15.55 lakh, the
project was abandoned. The reason for abandonment as stated by the Nodal Department
was due to the transfer of the project in-charge. The Nodal Department had not taken any
initiative to complete the project, which resulted not only in a wasteful expenditure of
& 15.55 lakh, but also deprived the intended benefit to the farmers of the State.

2.4.12.1 Hi-tech greenhouses at Mazitar (East District) and Birdang (West District)

The DPR contained the financial estimate of the projects only. Reasons and advantages of
the establishment of a hi-tech greenhouse instead of an ordinary green house were not
mentioned in the DPR.

Two hi-tech greenhouses at Mazitar (East district) and Birdang (West district) were
approved (November 2008) by the SLSC at a total cost of € 1.43 crore. The project was
implemented by the HCCDD. The objective of the establishment of the greenhouses was
to produce 50,000 numbers per annum quality seedling of oranges by each green house
for distribution to farmers. The construction of the greenhouses was awarded without
calling for tenders to the North Eastem Regional Agricultural Marketing Corporation
(NERAMC), a Government of India undertaking. The work commenced in February
2009 and was completed in October 2009 at a total cost of = 1.43 crore. The Department
paid the entire amount to the Corporation without keeping any security. However, joint
physical verification (September 2013) of the Birdang hi-tech greenhouse revealed that
the hi-tech greenhouse was running as an ordinary greenhouse as all the equipments
installed (October 2009) were non-functional. It was also given to understand that the
equipment tumed non-functional after six months of its installation. However, during
2012-13 (records relating to the previous years were not produced to Audit), it produced
30,000 seedlings out of which 21,550 seedlings were distributed to the farmers. The
Department however, had not initiated action to rectify the installed equipment due to
which the greenhouse was functioning as an ordinary greenhouse instead of hi-tech
greenhouse.

The performance of the greenhouse at Mazitar was good as it produced 95,000 seedlings
during 2012-13 as compared with its target of 50,000 per annum.

* A race or variety of a plant that has been created or selected intentionally and maintained through cultivation,



2.4.12.2 Establishment of hi-tech green house

Establishment of hi-tech green house at Bermiock (South) was approved by SLSC
(October 2009) at a total cost of € 86 lakh, of which हैं 40 lakh was approved by DAC and
the remaining portion was met from the State’s resources. The construction was awarded
to NERAMC and was completed in March 2011 at a total cost of 86 lakh. However,
joint physical verification conducted in September 2013 revealed that the greenhouse was
not working properly as there was no electricity connection since inception. As a result,
the production of orange seedlings during 2012-13 was only12,328 against the target of
50,000 per annum.

2.4.12.3 Non fulfilment of objective

SLSC approved (October 2009) the project ‘Promotion of high yields cross bred dairy
cattle progeny propagation and development’ to promote propagation of High Yielding
(HY) cross bred cows and its progeny in the State of Sikkim by inducting 1200 HY
crossbred cows with their progeny. The project was approved by the DAC at a total cost
of € 2.55 crore and was implemented by AHLFVSD. It was scheduled to be completed by
March 2013. The main components of the project were induction of HY cross bred
lactating cows with calves, induction of breeding bulls, vaccination against major
diseases, providing mineral mixture, de-worming, providing milk cans and capacity
building.

The DAC released ¥ 1.27 crore against the approved amount of & 2.55 crore and हैं 1.25
crore was incurred by the Department towards procurement and distribution of milching
cows, milk cans and cow feed. Neither the implementing Department nor the Nodal
Department had taken any steps for obtaining the balance funds of € 1.28 crore from GOI.
Moreover, the implementing Department had not carried out any monitoring of the
projects as envisaged in the guidelines of distribution of milching cows issued by the
Department.

For promotion and propagation of HY cross bred cows, the project also emphasised the
distribution of bulls of good breed and the same was also reiterated by SLSC. However,
the Department had not initiated any steps to provide the bulls as of August 2013.

The project was to be completed by March 2013. However, as of August 2013, only the
distribution of milching cows, milk cans and feed was implemented.

Thus, the Department not only failed in propagation of HY cows but also failed to
increase milk production as projected in the DPR.

contravention of guidelines and the DPRs.

distribution of milching cows.

2.4.12.4 Project not commenced

Project on E-pest surveillance for capturing data on pest and insects beneficial to the
farmers and transmitting it to National Centre for Integrated Pest Management through
internet was approved (October 2009) by SLSC at a total cost of 7 lakh. The main
objective of the project was effective pest management. However, the FSADD had not
started (September 2013) the project, for reasons not on record, thus depriving the
farmers of the intended benefit.

2.4.13.1 Monitoring and evaluation by Nodal Department
As per the guidelines, the SLSC would inter-alia be responsible for:
a) Monitoring the progress of the sanctioned projects/schemes;

b) Reviewing the implementation of the scheme’s objectives and ensuring that the
programmes are implemented in accordance with the guidelines laid down;

c) Commissioning/undertaking field studies to monitor the implementation of the
projects;

d) Initiating evaluation studies from time to time, as may be required;

e) Undertaking any other project of importance to the State’s Agriculture and allied
sectors.

In Sikkim, the SLSC was constituted in December 2007 and was required to hold

meetings once a quarter. Scrutiny of records revealed that during 2007-13, as against the

required 20 meetings, only 6 meetings had been held, which resulted in shortfall of 14

meetings, thereby affecting effectiveness in the implementation and monitoring of the

RKVY as required. Scrutiny of the minutes of the meetings made available to audit also

revealed that no discussion was held on issues like the status of projects, reasons for

delay in progress of work, or whether objectives of the Scheme were being met, etc. The

SLSC while sanctioning projects, made some recommendations in those six meetings

which were partially followed up as detailed in Appendix 2.4.2.

Physical and financial performance/achievements were not monitored with reference to
the targets and objectives of the projects which resulted in delay in completion of
projects, irregular implementation, non—achievement of objective of the projects, non-
viable projects being taken up etc. Further, the lack of monitoring was also reflected in
poor record keeping on the part of both the Nodal and implementing departments.



> Field inspection:
No field inspection was undertaken by the SLSC or by the officers of the Nodal

Department to monitor the implementation of various schemes for taking remedial
action to rectify the shortcomings when detected.

> Submission of quarterly physical and financial progress reports to the Union
ministry:

Effective monitoring of any programme depends mainly on timely submission of
reports and returns to the monitoring authorities. However, the implementing
Departments failed to submit any progress reports to the Nodal Department. The
Nodal Department consequently failed to submit the performance report in terms
of physical and financial achievements as well as outcomes to the Union Ministry.

Vv

Implementation of web-based management information system:

Various information of Stream I i.e. the sanctioned amount, expenditure, funds
released by State Government etc., were to be uploaded by the Nodal Department
in the website of RKVY. However, examination of website revealed that the
Department uploaded the details of Stream II as Stream I in some cases
erroneously and the expenditure figures were also incorrect. Further, the project
status and the outcomes were not updated regularly.

2.4.14.1 Agrarian status of the State

The net cultivated area of Sikkim is around 79,000 hectare. Since Sikkim is a hilly State,
the agriculture scenario is dominated by soil acidity, excess moisture during monsoon
period and moisture stress during post monsoon/winter, heavy weed infestation, low
temperature stress during winter, occurrence of hailstorm in pre kharif season and
incessant rain that causes leaching of nutrients and pesticides which favours high
incidence of diseases and pests that reduce crop yields. The main strength of the State is
its diverse climate for production of different quality of sub tropical to temperate fruits,
vegetables and flowers. The horticultural produce has high market potential in domestic
and distant markets.

The State Government has put forth a policy of organic farming with the target of making
Sikkim an Organic State by 2015. The Government framed organic action plan in the year
2003 and constituted the Sikkim State Organic Board.

2.4.14.2 Target and achievement as per SAP

There was no long term planning for growth of Agriculture and allied sectors in the State.
The State Agriculture Plan failed to address the holistic development of Agriculture and
allied sectors as it included only the projects under RKVY.



In the SAP, only the production of cereals, pulses, oilseeds and horticulture products was
projected for five years. Annual production of major agricultural products and targets set
in SAP are shown in the following charts:

2.4.14.3 Impact of the schemes

RKVY was launched with the aim of achieving four per cent annual growth in the
Agricultural sector during the XI Plan period, by ensuring holistic development of
Agriculture and allied sectors.

2.4.14.4 Contribution of Agriculture sector to GSDP

Annual growth rate of contribution of Agriculture and allied sector to GSDP was 3.92 per
cent during 2007-08 and ranged between 4.13 per cent and 4.86 per cent during 2008-09
and 2011-12. During 2012-13, it had increased to 6.65 per cent.

2.4.14.7 Evaluation

No evaluation on the implementation of the programmes and their impact on the State
were conducted either by the State Government or by any independent agency. Thus,
effectiveness of the programmes and their impact on the State was not assessed.



The planning process for the implementation of the scheme was not effective and failed to
address the basic requirement of convergence of various schemes for overall development
of the Districts. The DPRs were prepared without proper survey, investigation, market
study etc. Failure on the part of the Nodal Department to furnish the UCs in time also led
to the curtailment of funds. Large sums of advance remained unadjusted for a
considerable period. Projects were characterised by delay in implementation, overlapping
of the projects and implementation of non-viable projects. Monitoring was not in place
and evaluation was also not conducted. The State maintained the four per cent growth in
agricultural sector but the production of major crops had declined.

» The Nodal Department should ensure that survey, investigation, market study is
done while preparing the DPRs and it must contain the economic benefit, input-
output ratio;

> Utilisation Certificates should be submitted in time to ensure release of full
amount of funds allotted for the year;

» Projects should be implemented as per the approval of SLSC and with the
sanction of DAC;

> Monitoring mechanism should be strengthened to achieve the desired objectives
and to take corrective measures in case of any deviations; and

> Evaluation of the implementation of the programmes and their impact on the State
may be conducted to assess the effectiveness of the programmes.

Ministry of Development of North Eastern Region (DoONER), Government of India (GOI)
accorded administrative approval and financial sanction (February 2010) of % 29.71 crore
for ‘Construction of 66 Suspension Foot Bridges (SFBs)’ in Sikkim under Social &
Infrastructure Development Fund (SIDF) as part of = 500 crore package announced
(February 2008) for North East Region during 2008-09. The project was implemented by
the Rural Management and Development Department (RMDD), Government of Sikkim
through Zilla Panchayats (ZPs). As of March 2013, 51 SFBs (out of 66) were completed
and remaining 15 were under construction. Out of 51 SFBs completed, 21 were belatedly



completed recording a delay of more than six months (6-12 months- 6 SFBs; and beyond
1 year- 15 SFBs).

Thematic Audit of SFB under SIDF was taken up with the objective to assess whether the
selection of projects were need based and projects were implemented with due regard to
economy, efficiency and effectiveness. A total of 16 (out of 66) bridges were selected for
detailed scrutiny based on two stage sampling by adopting ‘Stratified’ and ‘Monetary
Unit Sampling’. The sample was later increased to 27 for increased assurance. Audit was
conducted during April- June 2013 through visits to the sites and test check of records.
maintained at the RMDD headquarters office, Gangtok and four Zilla Panchayats in the
districts.

The performance were benchmarked against the criteria stipulated in Sikkim Public
Works Code and Manual, terms and conditions set forth in the Administrative approval
and sanction orders by the DoNER, ete.

The audit findings are enumerated in the succeeding paragraphs:
2.5.3.1 Funds Management

A total of = 29.12 crore was released by DoNER of which expenditure of = 25.12 crore
was incurred upto March 2013, leaving an unspent balance of € 4 crore as shown below:

Table 2.5.1

(@ in crore)

. पु 1/8/2009-DoNER/SP

1/8/2009-DoNER/SP_

Total [29.12 | 25.12
Source: Fund release order of DoNER and Contingent Audit Register.

According to terms of sanction of DoNER, the funds were to be kept in a separate bank
account for the specific purpose of funding these constructions and to ensure ready
accessibility of the funds. It was, however, seen during audit that the funds amounting to
% 29.12 crore received from DoNER between March 2010 to December 2011 were kept
in the budget head (5054 - 04 - 101 - 36 -71 - 53 - Major works) by the State
Government. As a result, terms of sanction of DoNER was violated. Besides, this entailed
a loss of = 76.79 lakh on account of opportunity cost of interest on savings accounts even
when calculated at a low interest rate of four per cent per annum as detailed in Appendix
2.5.1 (a), (b), (०) and (d).



The Department stated (October 2013) that loss calculated by audit was presumptive, as
keeping the funds under budget head did not affect the progress of the work and even the
XIII Finance Commission recommendation discourages keeping the funds outside the
consolidated fund of the State. The reply was not acceptable as the funds were released by
DoNER and the Department was supposed to comply with the terms and conditions of
financial sanction set by the DoNER. It was also noticed in audit that the funds received
under other Programmes (Total Sanitation Campaign etc.) were kept in separate bank
accounts. On one hand, the Department had violated instruction of the funding agency by
giving reference of the XIII Finance Commission Report and on the other hand, it
continued to keep the funds in separate bank account for other schemes.

> Unauthorised expenditure

The terms of sanction (Para 3 (x)) by the DoNER stipulated (February 2010) that the
funds should be utilised strictly for the purpose for which they were sanctioned and that
no diversion of funds would be permitted.

Audit noticed that the implementing Department unauthorisedly utilised = 25.75 lakh
towards contingency in excess of the approved amount. Against the approved
contingency funds of % 50.78 lakh, the Department incurred हैं 76.54 lakh resulting in an
excess expenditure of = 25.75 lakh which was without any authorisation from the funding
agency.

The Department stated (October 2013) that their record indicated expenditure of 47.07
lakh was incurred from the contingency funds which was not acceptable as the figures
were without any supporting documents and also the Utilisation Certificate submitted to
DoNER showed an expenditure of € 76.54 from contingency funds.

2.5.3.2 Avoidable expenditure

Sikkim Public Works Manual (Para 4.1 read with 4.5) envisaged that the estimate of the
projects must inter-alia contain history indicating initiation and reasons leading to the
proposal, design indicating a description of the original proposals and those finally
adopted particularly with regard to location, setting and design specifications and
drawings. Hence, survey forms a vital component of the estimate of projects, especially
that of SFBs.

Audit noticed that although the original estimate was based on actual requirement and
detailed survey for identification of proper site, the assessment of span of the bridges
were inflated to obtain maximum assistance from the DoNER under SIDF. The following
cases will illustrate the points:

> SFB at Lower Borong

The SFB at Lower Borong was approved (February 2010) at an estimated cost of 91.26
lakh by DoNER with the objective of providing rural connectivity. According to the
technically approved estimate, the SFB over Rangit river at Lower Borong was to have
140 mtrs. span. The departmental engineer however informed (14 June 2010) that the site
was suitable for 105 mtrs. span and not 140 mtrs. as reflected in the approved estimate.



The field engineer was directed (18 June 2010) to locate an alternative site and submit
fresh proposal. Accordingly, fresh survey was conducted (22 June 2010) for search of
alternative site to accommodate 140 mtrs. Span. Physical verification revealed that the
bridge was constructed at a different site than originally approved.

> SFB at Dhargaon Phamtam

In case of Dhargaon Phamtam also, the 150 mtrs. span SFB was administratively
approved (February 2010) at an estimated cost of हैं 94.76 lakh to provide rural
connectivity. According to the field engineers, the site identified was suitable (21 June
2010) for 65 mtrs. span rather than 150 mtrs. span reflected in the approved estimate. The
field engineer was directed (21 June 2010) to identify alternative site to accommodate
150 mtrs. span SFB. Based on this direction, alternative site was located (25 June 2010) at
a distance of 50 mtrs downstream of the original site. Accordingly, the SFB was
constructed at an alternative site to accommodate 150 mtrs. span.

This not only indicated the defective assessment of length of span required but also
execution of the bridges at the site other than what was originally identified by the
Department and approved by the DoNER. The proposal for a span length, which was.
more than the actual length required at the proposed site, resulted in unnecessary
avoidable expenditure of % 36.97 lakh on construction of two bridges as detailed in
Appendix 2.5.2. Those extra funds expended could have been gainfully utilised
elsewhere to obtain maximum benefit in furtherance of SIDF objectives.

The Department stated (October 2013) that the field officials were instructed to re-verify
the site of construction and stick to the original site as far as possible. The instruction was
issued on the concept that the sanctioned scheme did not get diverted from the original
demand. The reply corroborates the audit observation as the spans in the original sites
were shorter than the spans of the bridges at changed sites.

2.5.3.3 Unauthorised expenditure

Terms of sanction (Para-3 (x)) stipulated that the funds would be incurred towards
specific projects and no diversion would be allowed. It was, however, noticed that the
approved span and site of the projects were altered without obtaining approval of the
funding agency (DoNER) as detailed below:

> SFB at Chuba in South Sikkim

The estimate of SFB over Kalej Khola at Chuba in South Sikkim was sanctioned
(February 2010) for € 33.08 lakh for 65 mtrs. span. The subsequent survey (June 2010) on.
receipt of sanction intimation revealed that the site was suitable for 25 mtrs. span and not
65 mtrs. span. The Department, however, constructed the SFB of 65 mtrs. span over
Bhutey Khola (instead of Kalej Khola) at an estimated cost of दैं 33.08 lakh (expenditure
₹ 29.66 lakh till date) without obtaining approval of DoNER. This led to unauthorised
expenditure of € 29.66 lakh (total committed expenditure हैं 33.08 lakh for the project) as
of March 2013.



> SFB over Rangit at Tokal

The ‘Construction of 145 mtrs. SFB over Rangit at Tokal’ was tendered (29 July 2010)
and awarded (5 January 2011) to the lowest bidder at 14.5 per cent above the estimated
cost with the condition for completing within twelve months (by January 2012).

Audit noticed that the span of the bridge was revised from 145 mtrs. to 100 mtrs. due to
change of location of the bridge which was not intimated to DoNER. The Department
misled the DoNER as the work order to the contractor and the Progress Report to the
DoNER continued to mention the earlier site and span (145 mtrs.).

This led to unauthorised expenditure of हैं 39.15 lakh (total committed expenditure of
% 1.31 crore for the project) as of March 2013. Though both the estimates were framed on
the basis of the same SOR (i.e. SOR 2006), the change in span of bridge from 145 mtrs to
100 mtrs had decreased the estimate of the bridge component by only हैं 599, Audit
analysis revealed that the estimate for 100 mtrs span was inflated by हैं 15.42 lakh as
detailed in Appendix 2.5.3.

The Department reiterated (October 2013) that the field officials were instructed to re-
verify the site of construction and stick to the original site as far as possible. The
instruction was issued on the concept that the sanctioned scheme did not get diverted
from the original demand. However, the fact remains that the original site was changed
without obtaining approval from the funding agency. Further, the Department had not
spelt out any reasons for inflated estimate of SFB over River Rangit at Tokal.

2.5.3.4 Tendering and award of works

The terms of sanction (Para-3(ii)) by DoNER envisaged (February 2010) that the State
Government ensure competitive bidding through wide publicity in the print media and in
the web site. The same was reiterated in the fund release orders as well.

Audit noticed that the Department did not comply with the terms of the sanction and
instead awarded works to Co-operative Societies in contravention to the above
stipulations.

The Department expressed their regret (October 2013) for not obtaining approval from

GOI before cancelling the competitive bidding as it would take long time to get approval
from GOI.

2.5.3.5 Excess expenditure on works

Sikkim Public Works Manual (Para-19.25) envisaged recovery of excess materials issued
to the contractors at double the issue rate.

Physical verification of 27 SFBs by Audit jointly with departmental officers revealed that
excess materials were i:

sued to the contractors. Joint physical verification and audit
analysis revealed that various items such as chequered plate (95 nos.), cross beams (65
nos.), and long beams (173 nos.) were issued in excess of what was actually utilised by
the contractors. The cost of the excess materials was recovered at normal issue rate (as



against the requirement of double the issue rate) leading to undue favour to the
contractors amounting to हैं 24.13 lakh (₹ 4.30 lakh towards fitting and fixing of excess
materials and % 19.84 lakh towards the recovery of cost of materials) as detailed in
Appendix 2.5.4,

The Department stated (October 2013) that the requirement of the bridge components
calculated in the estimate was unreasonably high due to presence of bugs in the software
“Yellow Jacket’ developed and used for preparing estimates of materials for the bridges.
Further, the Department stated that during the process of construction, some of the
materials get wasted which has to be considered as fait-accompli and is unavoidable.

The reply is not tenable as the estimates, though prepared by software was approved in
hardcopy and the same could have been checked. Moreover, undue favour was given to
the contractor by making payment for the works that was not executed at all. Further, the
Department failed to explain reasons for not recovering the cost of materials issued in
excess of requirement.

2.5.3.6 Execution of works

Sikkim Public Works Manual (Para- 4.17) provides that the design and drawings of the
bridge should not be changed without the specific approval of the Chief Engineer. Audit,
however, noticed that certain important parts were not installed as per the technically
approved estimate and design which resulted in the contractors deriving undue benefit as
detailed below:

> Free Board

According to the technically sanctioned approved design of the bridge, the height of the
railing is to be one meter and the minimum height between the main cable and railing
(called the free board) should be of 1.5 mtrs. to ensure that the lowest point of the main
cable is always 1.5 mtrs. above the railing.

Railing height of | गाए, | [Free Board of 1.5 mir,

Main cable



Physical verification by Audit along with the departmental officers revealed that in four
cases there was no free board at all (main cable was below the railing) as seen in the
photographs below’:

>» Guy Rope

The approved estimate incorporated a provision for Guy Rope to arrest swinging caused
by lateral forces like wind etc. and reinforce the strength of the bridge. Physical
verification done together by Audit and the departmental officers revealed (July 2013)
that 8 bridges did not have Guy Rope. This was despite a payment of % 0.42 lakh to the
contractor for fitting of Guy Rope. This not only indicated deviation from the originally
approved estimate but also the entries in the Measurement Book was not reliable.

> Angle Iron

Angle iron railing was provided in the approved estimate for enhanced safety. It was

however, noticed that while no angle iron was provided in two bridges (SFB over
Nimphu chu at Lachung and SFB over Teesta khola at Samardang), in three bridges
(SFB over Shingshore Khola, SFB over Hee Khola, and SFB over Kalej Khola),
adequate railing and support angle iron was not provided as shown in the photographs

“Image 2.5.1: SFB over Teesta Khola at Samardong; . Image 2.5.2: SEB over Rong Rong Khola at Kadamtam; Image 2.5.3: SFB
over Nimphu chu Lachung;. Image 2.5.4: SFB over Kalej Khola.

Singshore Khola; Image 2.5.8: SFB over Hee Khola; Image 2.5.9: SFB over Kalej Khola.



> Long Beams

According to the technically approved estimate, long beams were required to be placed in
three lines: two beams just below the railing and one in the middle. Physical verification
of bridges by the audit team along with the departmental officers revealed (July 2013)
that long beams in two bridges were placed in two lines (instead of three) as shown
below:

This led to construction of bridges in deviation from the originally approved design and
also compromised safety standards besides incurring payment on account of materials
(₹ 1.51 lakh) which were not utilised.

In agreeing to the observation, the Department stated (October 2013) that the technical
manpower employed for the work do not have every detailed knowledge on the
construction of suspension foot bridges and the same has to be considered a fait-accompli.

Audit of ‘Execution of Suspension Foot Bridges under SIDF’ by the Rural Management
& Development Department through ZPs revealed that out of 66 SFBs, 51 were
completed and remaining 15 were under construction. Out of the completed SFBs,
21 SFBs were completed recording a delay of more than six months. The results of Audit
on implementation of projects indicated defective survey and estimation, irregularity in
award of works, execution of works inconsistent with technically sanctioned approved
designs and estimates, excess and avoidable expenditure on construction of SFBs with
longer span than required, undue favour to contractors, excess issue of materials and
unauthorised expenditure.

The following are the recommendations for further improving the implementation of the
SFBs under SDF:

> The survey and investigation mechanism may be strengthened to ensure minimal
change in the estimate after obtaining approval and sanction of funds from GOI;



> Selection of site may be done only after proper site verification to ensure
execution of appropriate span and design;

> Financial management may be strengthened to avoid undue favour to the
contractors; and

> Ensure utilisation of the required materials in the execution of projects to ensure

safety and adherence to approved design.

On the basis of a proposal submitted (June 2011) by the Department, Government of
India (GOI) sanctioned (July 2011) ₹ 2.21 crore under the Centrally Sponsored Fodder
Development and Feed Development Scheme, under the component of “Grassland
Development including Grass Reserves”. The first installment of हैं. 110.50 lakh was
released in July 2011.

As per the terms of sanction, the work was to be executed at Condominium Property
Regimes (CPR)”, Gochar land*?/community land/waste land at हैं 85,000 per hectare in
260 hectares“. While sanctioning the funds, the GOI clearly stated that “Central Grant is
sanctioned on the condition that this will be utilised in the community/waste land as per
proposal submitted by the State Government.

Scrutiny of records revealed (April 2013) that instead of implementing the scheme
according to the terms of sanction and proposal submitted by itself to the GOI, which was
to be on Gochar land/community land/waste land, the Department implemented the
scheme in the fields owned by individual farmers through distribution of cash at the rate
of % 5,000 per individual farmer and expended हैं 70 lakh in the process of distribution of
cash to 1400 farmers. It was further noticed that the District Level Officers also spent
% 22.40 lakh towards distribution of manures, mineral mixture, etc., to those farmers,
aggregating a total expenditure of रं 92.40 lakh from the scheme funds during 2011-12.

Thus, the Department not only deviated from its own proposal, but also violated the
conditions/terms of sanction of GOI to utilise the funds for specific purpose of
implementation of Grassland Development including Grass Reserves Scheme in
community land/Gochar/CPR/waste land etc. For deviation from its own proposal against
which the sanction was obtained, the Department had also not obtained the requisite

® CPR means a big plot of land with ownership of the land as either tenants in common or by joint tenancy with their partners.

Gochar land means village grazing land/cow grazing land.
Against the proposal from the Department of implementation in 290 hectares for €4.24 crore.



approval from the GOI. Thus, the implementation was contrary to the letter and spirit of
the sanction and led to an unauthorised expenditure of € 92.40 lakh.

The Department stated (July 2013) that they had implemented the scheme (violating the
GOI guidelines) in the interest of farmers for enhancing fodder production in the lands of
individual farmers and based on the Audit observation, they would check such cases of
irregular expenditure in future.

The reply was not tenable as the utilisation certificate furnished (July 2012) by the
Department to the GOI stated that the amount was expended for the purpose for which it
was sanctioned. This certificate was however incorrect, as the amounts were not spent on.
works on CPR/Gochar land/community land/waste land but were spent on private lands.
Also, any expenditure not covered within the scope of the Centrally Sponsored Scheme
should have been borne by the State from its own resources. It may also be stated that the
relaxation for deviation from the terms of sanction in this respect was neither sought for
nor obtained from the GOI.

The Macro Management in Agriculture (MMA) scheme guidelines stipulates that the
maximum permissible assistance for Crop Production Programme under Integrated
Nutrient Management (INM) was to be 25 per cent of the cost limited to हैं 1,000 per
hectare. It was categorically stated that these amounts were not to be exceeded in order to
ensure that deliveries to the number of farmer beneficiaries are not curtailed.

Scrutiny of records revealed that the Department incurred & 249.44 lakh during 2010-11
and 2011-12 towards demonstration in 13,000 hectares of land under Integrated Nutrient
Management at an expenditure of € 1,918 per hectare. This expenditure was in excess of
the maximum permissible amount of 25 per cent of expenditure (© 62.36 lakh**) limited
to = 1,000 per hectare, resulting in unauthorised excess expenditure of % 187.08" lakh.
The Department had also not obtained the approval from GOI for the deviation from the
norms of MMA for incurring the excess expenditure of & 187.08 lakh from Central

Scheme funds.

Thus, the expenditure incurred by the Department in violation of the scheme guidelines
resulted in unauthorised expenditure of 187.08 lakh besides depriving many potential
farmer beneficiaries the admissible coverage under the scheme.

25 per cent of the expenditure = 25 per cent of €249.44 lakh = % 62.36 lakh
#.. % 62.36 lakh is less than हैं 130 lakh @ ₹ 1,000 per hectare and hence admissible. Excess expenditure (€ 249.44 lakh —% 62.36
lakh) = € 187.08 lakh,



The Department stated (August 2013) that the North Eastern States being hilly in nature,
annual washout of the plant nutrient is very high due to torrential monsoon season.
Further, State having gone into organic agriculture, the Department was compelled to
increase the application of plant nutrient in the soil to meet up the gap in organic fertilizer
and heavy loss of plant nutrient. It was further stated that GOI accorded approval @
% 3,000 per hectare in 2012-13 on the basis of work plan submitted by the Department.
However, fact remains that during the years 2010-11 and 2011-12, the amount approved
by GOI was @ हैं 1,000 per hectare and hence the Department, in violation of the scheme
guidelines, incurred unauthorised excess expenditure of € 187.08 lakh.

Analysis of Rates (AOR) is prepared by the Roads and Bridges Department (RBD) for
settling the rates for various items of works executed by the works departments. The
AORs also stipulate the (i) specification and the quantity of materials required to be used
in various items of works and (ii) deduction of 20 per cent of stone derived from the
excavation/hill cutting of hard/blasting rocks as usable stones for which stacking charges
@ हैं 7.02 per cubic metre was also allowed. Further, Sikkim Public Works (SPW)
Manual (Clause 45) stipulates that the contractor is required to submit all the
permits/royalty documents in original as proof of transportation of materials from the
designated quarry.

Scrutiny (February-March 2013) of the bills and statements of theoretical consumption
related to utilisation of stones during April 2008 to March 2013 pertaining to 14 works
revealed that payments to the contractors on account of carriage of stone were released by
the Department on the basis of quantity of stones claimed to have been utilised by the
contractors, without considering the quantities permissible as per the AOR. The
Department had not cross checked the quantity of stones required to be used as per the
specification in the AOR by taking into consideration the availability of 20 per cent of
stone from hard/blasting rock.

Moreover, it was seen that the contractors had not submitted the transit/royalty documents
as proof that the materials had actually been transported, to support the claim of the
quantity of stones transported, as required under the SPW Manual.

Audit observed that the actual requirement of stones to be transported was 84,246.65
cubic metre. Against this, the Department made payment for carriage of 1,20,995.30
cubic metre of stones. This resulted in excess payment of हैं 1.97 crore for the stated
transportation of 36,748.65 cubic metre of stones, which was over and above the quantity
required for the works, as per the AOR. Details are given in the Appendix 2.8.1.



Thus, failure on the part of the Department in exercising due diligence while checking the
quantum of stones for which payment was admissible, and also not taking into account
the 20 per cent derived from the cutting/hardblasting of usable stones as stipulated in the
AOR norms and ignoring the requirements of the SPW Manual, resulted in giving undue
benefit of € 1.97 crore to the contractors.

‘The matter was reported to the Department/Government (July 2013); their reply had not
been received (October 2013).

State of Sikkim had power availability of 161 Mega Watt’’ (MW) against the internal
consumption of 65 MW during 2010-2012. The surplus power was being sold by Energy
and Power Department (EPD), Government of Sikkim, through Power Exchange
Agreements“ and Bilateral Agreements” with Private Traders. During 2010-11, Energy
and Power Department (EPD) invited (January 2011) limited offers from three traders
(i.e., Tata Power Trading Co. Ltd (Tata), Global Energy Private Limited (Global) and
Mittal Processor Pvt. Ltd) to sell 30 MW of power on ‘Round The Clock (RTC)’ basis.
Tata offered the highest rate of = 4.19%" per kwh against the second highest offer of
& 3.90 per kwh from Global.

EPD accepted (January 2011) the second highest bid offer of ¥ 3.90 per kwh from Global
as against the highest bid of Tata on the grounds that: (i) in the past, the bidder backed out
in the last moment; (ii) their delivery point being Eastern Region periphery would cause
additional cost of three per cent towards power transmission and (iii) their offer was valid
only upto October 2011 as against March 2012 fixed by the Department.

Scrutiny revealed (November 2012) that while rejecting the offer of Tata, the EPD did not
consider the fact that out of the 14 traders engaged in the country during 2009-10 in short
term trading of power, Tata was amongst the top three traders, whose share of electricity
trading during 2009-10 was 9.83 per cent. As against this, the Global did not exist in the
list of 14 traders, which was indicative of the fact that the firm (Global) was not as
experienced and reputed as Tata in the power trading. The incompetence of Global was
further established by the fact that it subsequently defaulted in clearing its dues to EPD.
Further, even after considering the cost of applicable transmission charges at around three
Including 68 MW purchased from Central Public Sector Undertakings through allocation by MOP, 601, 75 MW free power
availed from hydro plants of NHPC commissioned in Sikkim and 16 MW from Others, hesides 2 MW generated by the State's
own generating plants.

A bilateral contract in an electricity market is an agreement between a willing buyer and a willing seller to exchange electricity

As regards the periodicity of offer (up to October 2011) by Tata, it is pertinent to mention
that the issue should have been resolved by EPD through negotiations considering the
reputation and higher price offered by Tata.

Due to selection of Global instead of Tata, the EPD had to incur a loss 01₹ 2.55 crore on
sale of 87.84 million units of power during April 2011 to July 2011.

It was also noticed that while accepting the bid of Global for sale of power, no formal
agreement or Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) was entered into with Global so as
to safeguard the interest of the Government. In the absence of any legally binding
agreement, the bill payment security mechanism i.e., opening of Letter of Credit (LC),
entering into tripartite agreement with the banker of the firm, etc. was also not ensured.
Due to failure of EPD in ensuring payment security mechanism, dues to the extent of
%7.14 crore remained outstanding for recovery from Global as of October 2012.
Consequently, the EPD sustained loss of interest to the tune of € 1.01 crore™ for the
period from June 2011 to October 2012 on the said unrecovered dues.

Thus, the decision of EPD to reject the offer of the highest bidder (Tata) with reputed
background in power trading coupled with absence of a formal agreement for sale of
power with Global resulted in an avoidable loss of = 2.55 crore towards sale proceeds,
besides the loss of interest of 1.01 crore on non-recovery of dues of € 7.14 crore.

In reply, the EPD stated (September 2013) that the dealings with Tata were not reliable
considering their failure in drawal of power to the full extent during December 2010 to
March 2011 as per agreement with them and that the defaults made by Global in payment
of dues were on account of defaults in payment by the end users.

The justification regarding non-reliability of Tata as put forth by the EPD for rejecting the
bid of Tata was contradictory to its own decision of considering Tata for invitation of
limited bid offers for sale of power. The possibilities of backing out by Tata in the last
moment, as contended by the EPD could have also been avoided by calling for earnest
money deposit and bank guarantee mandatorily from the bidders. As regards the reasons
put forth for defaults in payment by Global, it is stated that the same do not justify renewal
of purchase agreement with Global as the inefficiencies in recovery of dues by Global
cannot be linked with the obligation of Global for timely payment of dues of the EPD.
